22:                                                 the notion of colouring the square of graphs and of cyclic colouring of graphs embedded
23:                                                 in a surface. We prove a general result which implies asymptotic versions of Wegner’s
24:                                                 and Borodin’s Conjecture on the planar version of these two colourings. Using a recent
25:                                                 approach of Havet et al., we reduce the problem to edge-colouring of multigraphs and
26:                                                 then use Kahn’s result that the list chromatic index is close to the fractional chromatic
27:                                                 index.
28:                                                     Our results are based on a strong structural lemma for graphs embedded in a surface
29:                                                 which also implies that the size of a clique in the square of a graph of maximum degree ∆
30:                                                 embeddable in some fixed surface is at most 32 ∆ plus a constant.

94:    Following Wegner’s Conjecture on colouring the square of planar graphs ( see also next
95: subsection ), we propose the following conjecture.
96: 
97: Conjecture 1.1
98: There exist constants c1 , c2 , c3 such that for all planar graphs G and A, B ⊆ V we have
99:                                       
100:       χ(G; A, B) ≤ 32 ∆(G; A, B) + c1 ;
101:                                       
102:      ch(G; A, B) ≤ 23 ∆(G; A, B) + c2 ;
103:                                       
104:      ch(G; A, B) ≤ 23 ∆(G; A, B) + 1,                   if ∆(G; A, B) ≥ c3 .
105: 
106: If A = ∅ ( hence ∆(G; A, B) = 0 ) and B = V , then the Four Colour Theorem means that
107: the smallest possible value for c1 is four; while the fact that planar graphs are always 5-list
108: colourable but not always 4-list colourable, shows that the smallest possible value for c2 is
109: five.
110:     Our main result is that Conjecture 1.1 is asymptotically correct. In fact, we can prove a
111: more general asymptotic version, which holds for general surfaces.
112: 
113: Theorem 1.2
114: Let S be a fixed surface, G a graph embeddable in S, and A, B ⊆ V . Then ch(G; A, B) ≤
115: (1 + o(1)) 23 ∆(G; A, B).
116:     In other words, for all ε > 0, there exists DS,ε , so that for all D ≥ DS,ε we have : If G is
117: a graph embeddable in S, with A, B ⊆ V so that ∆(G; A, B) ≤ D, and L is a list assignment
118: so that each vertex v in B gets a list L(v) of at least 32 + ε D colours, then there exists an
119: (A, B)-colouring of G in which the vertices in B are assigned colours from their own lists.
120: 
121: A trivial lower bound for the ( list ) chromatic number of a graph G is the clique number ω(G),
172: ∆2 + 1 colours.
173:     Regarding the chromatic number of the square of a planar graph, Wegner [31] posed the
174: following conjecture ( see also the book of Jensen and Toft [14, Section 2.18] ), suggesting that
175: for planar graphs far less than ∆2 + 1 colours suffice.
176: 
177: Conjecture 1.6 ( Wegner [31] )                      
178:                                                      7,        if ∆ = 3,
179: For a planar graph G of maximum degree ∆ : χ(G2 ) ≤   ∆  + 5,   if 4 ≤ ∆ ≤ 7,
180:                                                      3 
181:                                                        2 ∆ + 1, if ∆ ≥ 8.
182: 
183: Wegner also gave examples showing that these bounds would be tight. For even ∆ ≥ 8, these
184: examples are sketched in Figure 1(a).
185:     The graph in the picture has maximum degree 2 k and yet all the vertices except z are
186: pairwise adjacent in its square. Hence to colour these 3 k + 1 vertices, we need at least
187: 3 k + 1 = 32 ∆ + 1 colours. Note that the same arguments also show that the graph G in the
188: picture has ω(G2 ) = 32 ∆ + 1.
189:     Kostochka and Woodall [19] conjectured that for every square of a graph the chromatic
190: number equals the list chromatic number. This conjecture and Wegner’s one together imply
191: the conjecture that for planar graphs G with ∆ ≥ 8 we have ch(G2 ) ≤ 23 ∆ + 1.
192:     The first upper bound on χ(G2 ) for planar graphs in terms of ∆, χ(G2 ) ≤ 8 ∆ − 22,
193: was implicit in the work of Jonas [15]. This bound was later improved by Wong [32] to

288: 1.2   Cyclic Colourings of Embedded Graphs
289: Given a surface S and a graph G embeddable in S, we denote by GS that graph with a
290: prescribed embedding in S. If the surface S is the sphere, we talk about a plane graph GP .
291: The size ( number of vertices in its boundary ) of a largest face of GS is denoted by ∆∗ (GS ).
292:     A cyclic colouring of an embedded graph GS is a vertex colouring of G such that any
293: two vertices incident to the same face have distinct colours. The minimum number of colours
295: ber χ∗ (GS ). This concept was introduced for plane graphs by Ore and Plummer [25], who
296: also proved that for a plane graph GP we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 2 ∆∗ . Borodin [3] ( see also Jensen
297: and Toft [14, page 37] ) conjectured the following.
298: 
299: Conjecture 1.11 ( Borodin [3] )                                     
300: For a plane graph GP of maximum face size ∆∗ we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 23 ∆∗ .
301: 
302: The bound in this conjecture is best possible. Consider the plane graph depicted in Fig-
303: ure 1(b) : it has 3 k vertices and has three faces of size∆∗ = 2 k. Since all pairs of vertices
304: have a face they are both incident with, we need 3 k = 32 ∆∗ colours in a cyclic colouring.
305:    Borodin [3] also proved Conjecture 1.11 for ∆∗ = 4. For general values of ∆∗ , the original
306: bound χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 2 ∆∗ of Ore and Plummer [25] was improved by Borodin et al. [6] to
307: 
308:                                                6
309: 
310:                  
311: χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 59 ∆∗ . The  5 best known upper bound in the general case is due to Sanders and
312:               ∗   P
313: Zhao [27] : χ (G ) ≤ 3 ∆ .   ∗
314: 
315:      Although Wegner’s and Borodin’s Conjectures seem to be closely related, nobody has ever
316: been able to bring to light a direct connection between them. Most of the results approaching
317: these conjectures use the same ideas, but up until this point ( as far as the authors know ) no
318: one had proved a general theorem implying both a result on the colouring of the square and
319: a result on the cyclic colouring of plane graphs ( let alone on embedded graphs ).
320:      In order to show that our Theorem 1.2 provides an asymptotically best possible upper
321: bound for the cyclic chromatic number for a graph G with some fixed embedding GS , we need
322: some extra notation. For each face f of GS , add a vertex xf and call XF the set of vertices
323: that were added to G. For any face f of GS , and any vertex v incident with f , add an edge
324: between v and xf . We denote by GF the graph obtained from GS by this construction, so
325: V (GF ) = V (G) ∪ XF . Observe that a (list) (XF , V (G))-colouring of GF is exactly a cyclic
326: (list) colouring of GS and that ∆(GF ; XF , V (G)) = ∆∗ (GS ). We get the following corollary
327: of Theorem 1.2.

1373: generalise our main theorem and the theorem above in the following way. For A, B ⊆ V , let
1374: k(G; A, B) be the maximum of |NB (a1 ) ∩ NB (a2 )| over all a1 , a2 ∈ A, a1 6= a2 .
1375: Conjecture 5.2
1376: Let S be a fixed surface. Then there exists a constant cS so that for all graphs G embeddable
1377: on S, and A, B ⊆ V , we have
1378:       ch(G; A, B) ≤ ∆(G; A, B) + k(G; A, B) + cS .
1379: This conjecture would fit with our current proof of Theorem 1.4, the main part of which
1380: is a reduction of the original problem to a list edge-colouring problem. For this approach,
1381: Shannon’s Theorem
1382:                 3     [29]
1383:                           that a multigraph with maximum degree ∆ has an edge-colouring
1384: using at most 2 ∆(G) colours, forms a natural base for the bounds conjectured in Conjec-
1385: ture 1.1. If the relation between colouring the square of graphs embeddable on a fixed surface
1386: and edge-colouring multigraphs holds in a stronger sense, then Conjecture 5.2 forms a logical
1387: extension of Vizing’s Theorem [30] that a multigraph with maximum degree ∆ and maximum
1388: edge-multiplicity µ has an edge-colouring with at most ∆ + µ colours.
1389:     In Borodin et al. [5], a weaker version of Conjecture 5.2 for cyclic colouring of plane graphs
1390: was proved. Recall that if G is a plane graph, then ∆∗ is the maximum number of vertices in
1391: a face. Let k∗ denote the maximum number of vertices that two faces of G have in common.
1392: Theorem 5.3 ( Borodin, Broersma, Glebov & van den Heuvel [5] )
1393: For a plane graph G with ∆∗ ≥ 4 and k∗ ≥ 4 we have χ∗ (G) ≤ ∆∗ + 3 k∗ + 2.

1396: 6    Kahn’s Work on List Edge-Colourings
1397: As mentioned earlier, Theorem 2.8 is not explicitly stated in [17], but is implicit in the proof
1398: of the main result of that paper. In this final section, we give an overview of how the theorem
1399: can be obtained from the ideas in Kahn’s paper.

287: 1.2   Cyclic Colourings of Embedded Graphs
288: Given a surface S and a graph G embeddable in S, we denote by GS that graph with a
289: prescribed embedding in S. If the surface S is the sphere, we talk about a plane graph GP .
290: The order of a face of GS is the number of vertices in its boundary; the maximum order of a
291: face of GS is denoted by ∆∗ (GS ).
292:     A cyclic colouring of an embedded graph GS is a vertex colouring of G such that any
293: two vertices in the boundary of the same face have distinct colours. The minimum number
294: of colours required in a cyclic colouring of an embedded graph is called the cyclic chromatic
295: number χ∗ (GS ). This concept was introduced for plane graphs by Ore and Plummer [26],
296: who also proved that for a plane graph GP we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 2 ∆∗ . Borodin [3] ( see also
297: Jensen and Toft [14, page 37] ) conjectured the following.
298: 
299: Conjecture 1.9 ( Borodin [3] )                                       
300: For a plane graph GP of maximum face order ∆∗ we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 32 ∆∗ .
301: 
302: The bound in this conjecture is best possible. Consider the plane graph depicted in Fig-
303: ure 1(b) : It has 3 k vertices and has three faces of order ∆∗ =
304:                                                                  2 k. Since all pairs of vertices
305: have a face they are both incident with, we need 3 k = 23 ∆∗ colours in a cyclic colouring.
306:    Borodin [3] also proved Conjecture 1.9 for ∆∗ = 4. For general values of ∆∗ , the original
307: bound χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 2 ∆∗ of Ore and Plummer [26] was improved by Borodin et al. [6] to
308: 
309:                                                 6
310: 
311:                 
312: χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 59 ∆∗ . The   5 best known upper bound in the general case is due to Sanders and
313:               ∗  P
314: Zhao [29] : χ (G ) ≤ 3 ∆ .    ∗
315: 
316:     Although Wegner’s and Borodin’s Conjectures seem to be closely related, nobody has ever
317: been able to bring to light a direct connection between them. Most of the results approaching
318: these conjectures use the same ideas, but up until this point no one had proved a general
319: theorem implying both a result on the colouring of the square and a result on the cyclic
320: colouring of plane graphs ( let alone on embedded graphs ).
321:     In order to show that our Theorem 1.2 provides an asymptotically best possible upper
322: bound for the cyclic chromatic number for a graph G with some fixed embedding GS , we need
323: some extra notation. For each face f of GS , add a vertex xf . For any face f of GS and any
324: vertex v in the boundary of f , add an edge between v and xf , and denote by GF the graph
325: obtained from GS by this construction. Note that the vertex set of GF consists of V (G) and
326: all the new vertices xf , for f a face of GS . Define a Σ-system ΣF for GF as follows : For
327: each vertex v ∈ V (G), let ΣF (v) = ∅. For each vertex xf , let ΣF (xf ) be the vertices in the
328: boundary of f . Observe that a ( list ) ΣF -colouring of GF colours the vertices of G in a way
329: required for a cyclic ( list ) colouring of GS , and that ∆(GF ; ΣF ) = ∆∗ (GS ).
1386: over all pairs u, v of distinct vertices.

1388: Conjecture 5.2
1389: Let S be a fixed surface. Then there exists a constant cS such that for all graphs G embeddable
1390: on S, with a Σ-system, we have
1391: 
1392:       ch(G; Σ) ≤ ∆(G; Σ) + k(G; Σ) + cS .
1393: 
1394: This conjecture would fit with our current proof of Theorem 1.2, the main part of which
1395: is a reduction of the original problem to a list edge-colouring problem. For this approach,
1396: Shannon’s Theorem
1397:                       [31]
1398:                           that a multigraph with maximum degree ∆ has an edge-colouring
1399: using at most 23 ∆(G) colours, forms a natural base for the bounds conjectured in Conjec-
1400: ture 1.1. If the relation between colouring the square of graphs embeddable on a fixed surface
1401: and edge-colouring multigraphs holds in a stronger sense, then Conjecture 5.2 forms a logical
1402: extension of Vizing’s Theorem [32] that a multigraph with maximum degree ∆ and maximum
1403: edge-multiplicity µ has an edge-colouring with at most ∆ + µ colours.
1404:     In Borodin et al. [5], a weaker version of Conjecture 5.2 for cyclic colouring of plane graphs
1405: was proved. Recall that if GP is a plane graph, then ∆∗ is the maximum number of vertices in
1406: a face. Let k∗ denote the maximum number of vertices that two faces of GP have in common.
1407: 
1408: Theorem 5.3 ( Borodin, Broersma, Glebov & Van den Heuvel [5] )
1409: For a plane graph GP with ∆∗ ≥ 4 and k∗ ≥ 4 we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ ∆∗ + 3 k∗ + 2.

284: 1.2   Cyclic Colourings of Embedded Graphs
285: Given a surface S and a graph G embeddable in S, we denote by GS that graph with a
286: prescribed embedding in S. If the surface S is the sphere, we talk about a plane graph GP .
287: The order of a face of GS is the number of vertices in its boundary; the maximum order of a
288: face of GS is denoted by ∆∗ (GS ).
289:     A cyclic colouring of an embedded graph GS is a vertex colouring of G such that any
290: two vertices in the boundary of the same face have distinct colours. The minimum number
291: of colours required in a cyclic colouring of an embedded graph is called the cyclic chromatic
292: number χ∗ (GS ). This concept was introduced for plane graphs by Ore and Plummer [26],
293: who also proved that for a plane graph GP we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 2∆∗ . Borodin [3] (see also
294: Jensen and Toft [14, page 37]) conjectured the following.
295: 
296: Conjecture 1.9 (Borodin [3])                                         
297: For a plane graph GP of maximum face order ∆∗ we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ 32 ∆∗ .
298: 
299: The bound in this conjecture is best possible. Consider the plane graph depicted in Fig-
300: ure 1(b): It has 3k vertices and has three faces of order   ∗
301:                                                          3∆ ∗ = 2k. Since all pairs of vertices
302:  have a face they are both incident with, we need 3k = 2 ∆ colours in a cyclic colouring.
303:     Borodin [3] also proved Conjecture 1.9 for ∆∗ = 4. For general values of ∆∗ , the original
304:          ∗   P       ∗                                                                  ∗   P
305: bound
306:   9  ∗

308:   5 ∆ . The 5 best
309:                    known upper bound in the general case is due to Sanders and Zhao [29]:
310:   ∗   P
311: χ (G ) ≤ 3 ∆ .  ∗

313:     Although Wegner’s and Borodin’s Conjectures seem to be closely related, nobody has ever
314: been able to bring to light a direct connection between them. Most of the results approaching
315: these conjectures use the same ideas, but up until this point no one had proved a general
316:

319: theorem implying both a result on the colouring of the square and a result on the cyclic

322: bound for the cyclic chromatic number for a graph G with some fixed embedding GS , we need
323: some extra notation. For each face f of GS , add a vertex xf . For any face f of GS and any
324: vertex v in the boundary of f , add an edge between v and xf , and denote by GF the graph

1415: Conjecture 5.2
1416: Let S be a fixed surface. Then there exists a constant cS such that for all graphs G embeddable
1417: on S, with a Σ-system, we have
1418: 
1419:       ch(G; Σ) ≤ ∆(G; Σ) + k(G; Σ) + cS .
1420: 
1421: This conjecture would fit with our current proof of Theorem 1.2, the main part of which
1422: is a reduction of the original problem to a list edge-colouring problem. For this approach,
1423: Shannon’s Theorem
1424:                 3     [31]
1425:                           that a multigraph with maximum degree ∆ has an edge-colouring
1426: using at most 2 ∆(G) colours, forms a natural base for the bounds conjectured in Conjec-
1427: ture 1.1. If the relation between colouring the square of graphs embeddable on a fixed surface
1428: and edge-colouring multigraphs holds in a stronger sense, then Conjecture 5.2 forms a logical
1429: extension of Vizing’s Theorem [32] that a multigraph with maximum degree ∆ and maximum
1430: edge-multiplicity µ has an edge-colouring with at most ∆ + µ colours.
1431:     In Borodin et al. [5], a weaker version of Conjecture 5.2 for cyclic colouring of plane graphs
1432: was proved. Recall that if GP is a plane graph, then ∆∗ is the maximum number of vertices in
1433: a face. Let k∗ denote the maximum number of vertices that two faces of GP have in common.
1434: 
1435: Theorem 5.3 (Borodin, Broersma, Glebov & Van den Heuvel [5])
1436: For a plane graph GP with ∆∗ ≥ 4 and k∗ ≥ 4 we have χ∗ (GP ) ≤ ∆∗ + 3k∗ + 2.
1437: 
1438: Σ-Colouring and Minor-Closed Classes.
1439: It seems natural to expect that our work on graphs embeddable in a fixed surface can be
1440: extended to arbitrary proper minor-closed classes of graphs. Compare our main Theorem 1.2
1441: with Theorem 1.6, the main result from [10]. But there exist some obstacles to a direct
1442: generalisation.

22:                                                concept generalises the notion of colouring the square of graphs and of cyclic colouring
23:                                                of graphs embedded in a surface. We prove a general result for graphs embeddable in a
24:                                                fixed surface, which implies asymptotic versions of Wegner’s and Borodin’s Conjecture on
25:                                                the planar version of these two colourings. Using a recent approach of Havet et al., we
26:                                                reduce the problem to edge-colouring of multigraphs, and then use Kahn’s result that the
27:                                                list chromatic index is close to the fractional chromatic index.
28:                                                    Our results are based on a strong structural lemma for graphs embeddable in a fixed
29:                                                surface, which also implies that the size of a clique in the square of a graph of maximum
30:                                                degree ∆ embeddable in some fixed surface is at most 32 ∆ plus a constant.

175: following conjecture (see also the book of Jensen and Toft [14, Section 2.18]), suggesting that
176: for planar graphs far less than ∆2 + 1 colours suffice.

184: 
185: Wegner also gave examples showing that these bounds would be tight. For ∆ ≥ 8 even, these
186: examples are sketched in Figure 1(a). The graph in the picture has maximum degree 2k and
187: yet all the vertices except z are pairwise adjacent in its square. Hence to colour these 3k + 1
188: vertices, we need at least 3k + 1 = 23 ∆ + 1 colours. Note that the same arguments also show
189: that the graph G in the picture has ω(G2 ) = 32 ∆ + 1.
190:     Kostochka and Woodall [19] conjectured that for the square of any graph, the chromatic
191: number equals the list chromatic number. This conjecture and Wegner’s one together imply
192: the conjecture that for planar graphs G with ∆ ≥ 8, we have ch(G2 ) ≤ 23 ∆ + 1.
193:     The first upper bound on χ(G2 ) for planar graphs in terms of ∆, χ(G2 ) ≤ 8∆ − 22,
194: was implicit in the work of Jonas [15]. This bound was later improved by Wong [34] to
195: χ(G2 ) ≤ 3∆ + 5 and then by Van den Heuvel and McGuinness [13] to χ(G2 ) ≤ 2∆ + 25.
196: Better bounds were then obtained for large values of ∆. It was shown by Agnarsson and
197: Halldórsson [1] that for ∆ ≥ 750 we have χ(G2 ) ≤ ⌈ 95 ∆⌉ + 1; the same bound was proved for
198: ∆ ≥ 47 by Borodin et al. [4]. Finally, the best known upper bound so far has been obtained

FILE arxiv.org_pdf_1002.4187v1.txt
1:                                                  ABOUT CERTAIN POLYNOMIALS RELATED TO THE
2:                                                      RAZUMOV–STROGANOV CONJECTURE
3: 
4: 
5:                                                                  TIAGO FONSECA AND PHILIPPE NADEAU
6: 
7: 
8:                                                   Abstract. The Razumov–Stroganov conjecture states that two families of
9:                                                   numbers, both indexed by noncrossing matchings of n points, are the same:
10:                                                   on the one hand, the number of Fully Packed Loop configurations on a grid
11: 
12: 
13: 
14: 
15: arXiv:1002.4187v1 [math.CO] 22 Feb 2010
16:                                                   with a given matching, and on the other hand, the groundstate components
17:                                                   in the O(1)–loop model. When considering matchings with m nested arches,
18:                                                   both families of numbers were shown in previous works to be polynomial in m.
19:                                                   We describe here several conjectures about these polynomials: they concern
20:                                                   an explicit description of all real roots, special values of these polynomials, and
21:                                                   the positivity of the coefficients. The conjectures, which are of a combinatorial
22:                                                   nature, are supported by numerical evidence and checked to be consistent with
23:                                                   several known facts about the polynomials. Special cases of the conjectures
24:                                                   about roots and coefficients are proved. An extension of the conjectures for
25:                                                   the case of an extra parameter τ is finally given.
26: 
27: 
28: 
29: 
30:                                                                                  Introduction
31:                                              The Razumov–Stroganov conjecture [16] is a certain equality between, on the one
32:                                           hand, combinatorially defined quantities called Fully Packed Loop configurations,
33:                                           and on the other hand, components of the groundstate vector of the Hamilton-
34:                                           ian in the O(1)–loop model. Both quantities are indexed by noncrossing, perfect
35:                                           matchings π of 2n points (cf. definition in Section 1.1). FPL configurations with
36:                                           associated matching π will be denoted Aπ , while the corresponding components of
37:                                           the groundstate vector in the O(1)–loop model are written Ψπ . The Razumov–
38:                                           Stroganov conjecture states then that Aπ = Ψπ for any π. One of the difficulties
39:                                           of attacking the conjecture is that the quantities in question are not easy to eval-
40:                                           uate. Admittedly, there exist formulas for the Ψπ , which are recalled briefly in
41:                                           Section 2.3, but these are already complicated; and as for the Aπ , there is basically
42:                                           no known way to enumerate them in general. The most general class of matchings
43:                                           for which the Razumov–Stroganov Conjecture is proved can be found in [23].
44: 
45:                                              The goal of this article is to exhibit some surprising properties of these numbers
46:                                           when one studies matchings with nested arches, introduced by Zuber [25]. Consider
47:                                           the matchings (π)p = (· · · (π) · · · ), which means that there are p nested arches above
48:                                           the matching π. It was then observed in [25], and subsequently proved in [2, 9],
49:                                           that the quantities A(π)p and Ψ(π)p are polynomial in p. We introduce then the
50:                                           polynomials Aπ (m) and Ψπ (m) which correspond to the previous quantities when
51:                                           the indeterminate m is specialized to the nonnegative integer p.
52:                                              The main conjectures of this paper concern properties of these polynomials.
53:                                           What these conjectures imply is that there is some combinatorial reciprocity going
54:                                           on, in the sense that the values of the polynomials for negative specializations of
55:                                           the parameter m show a lot of structure: we believe that they should count certain
56:                                           objects. We gather some evidence for the conjectures, and prove some special cases
57:                                           (Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 5.1). In the O(1)–loop model, one can in fact define
58:                                           bivariate polynomials Ψ(τ, m) that coincide with Ψ(m) at τ = 1; it turns out that
59:                                                                                           1
60: 
61: 2                         TIAGO FONSECA AND PHILIPPE NADEAU
62: 
63: 
64: most of our conjectures admit a natural generalizazion in this context also, which
65: in some sense is more evidence for the original conjectures.
66:     We believe these conjectures can help us understand better the numbers Aπ and
67: Ψπ . Moreover, our work on these conjectures has some interesting byproducts:
68: first, the conjectured root multiplicities of the polynomials Xπ (m) have nice com-
69: binatorial descriptions in terms of π (see Section 3.1). Then, from the proof of
70: Theorem 4.1, we deduce some nice formulas about products of hook lengths of par-
71: titions (Proposition 4.3). Also, the proof of Theorem 5.1 involves the introduction
72: of a new multivariate integral.
73:    Let us give a detailed outline of this article, where π will refer to a matching
74: with n arches. In Section 1, we define the quantities Aπ and Ψπ , and formulate the
75: Razumov–Stroganov conjecture. We follow this in Section 2 by defining the main
76: objects of our study, the polynomials Aπ (m) and Ψπ (m). It is also recalled how to
77: approach the computation of these polynomials, based on previous works.
78:    These polynomials are the object of our main conjectures in Section 3. They
79: deal with real roots of the polynomials (Conjecture 3.2), their values at negative
80: integers between 1 − n and −1 (Conjecture 3.6), evaluations at −n (Conjecture 3.9)
81: and finally the positivity of the coefficients (Conjecture 3.10). We give also some
82: evidence for these conjectures, the most important one being that they have been
83: checked extensively for matchings with n ≤ 5 for the FPL case, and n ≤ 8 for the
84: O(1) model.
85:    The next two sections address particular cases of some of the conjectures: in
86: Section 4, we are concerned with the subleading term of the polynomials compu-
87: tation. The main result, Theorem 4.1, shows that this is a positive number both
88: for Aπ (m) and Xπ (m), and that the coefficients are in fact the same, thereby giv-
89: ing some more support for the Razumov–Stroganov Conjecture. Note that in the
90: process, we derive some nice formulas concerning hook lengths (Proposition 4.3).
91: Section 5 is concerned uniquely with the numbers Ψπ (m), and the main result there
92: is that if {1, 2n} is not an arch in π, then Ψπ (−1) = 0; this is a special case of
93: Conjecture 3.2. The proof relies on the multivariate polynomial extension of Ψπ ,
94: the main properties of which are recalled briefly. Finally, Section 6 is concerned
95: with Ψπ (τ, m), which are bivariate polynomials which specialize to Ψπ (m) when
96: τ = 1. It turns out that the conjectures of Section 3 generalize then in a very
97: satisfying way.
98:    We finally give two appendices. Appendix A gives a proof of the technical result
99: Theorem 3.1, while Appendix B lists some data about the polynomials Xπ (m).
100: 
101:                                        1. Definitions
102:    We first introduce matchings and different notions related to them. Then we
103: describe Fully Packed Loop configurations, and end up with the O(1)–loop model.
104: 
105: 1.1. Matchings. A matching1 of size n is defined as a set of n disjoint pairs of
106: integers {1, . . . , 2n}, which are noncrossing in the sense that if {i, j} and {k, l} are
107: two pairs with i < j and k < l, then it is forbidden to have i < k < j < l or
115: the only kind of matchings we will encounter so there will be no possible confusion.
116: 
117:             POLYNOMIALS AND THE RAZUMOV–STROGANOV CONJECTURE                            3
118: 
119: 
120:       • A well-formed sequence of parentheses, also called parenthesis word. If the
121:         point is connected to the left (respectively to the right), then this is encoded
122:         by an opening parenthesis (resp. by a closing parenthesis);
123:                                                ⇔ ()(())
124:       • A Dyck Path, which is a path between (0, 0) and (2n, 0) with steps NE
125:         (1, 1) and SE (1, −1) that never goes under the horizontal line y = 0. An
126:         opening parenthesis corresponds to a NE step, and a closing one to a SE
127:         step;
128:                                 ()(()) ⇔
232:     The original proofs can be found in [22, 12] for An , and [13] for AVn .

239: an integer, and define a state as a formal linear combination of matchings of size n.
240:    Let ei be the operator on matchings which creates a new arch at (i, i + 1), and

269: cause of the Razumov–Stroganov conjecture.

271: 1.4. The Razumov–Stroganov conjecture. A simple computation shows that
272:             Ψ       =2              Ψ          =2           Ψ       =1

279:   Razumov and Stroganov noticed in 2001 that this seems to hold in general:
280: Conjecture 1.5 ([16]). The groundstate components of the O(1) loop model count
281: the number of FPL configurations: for any matching π,
282:                                      Ψ π = Aπ .
283:    Special cases have been proved, the most general one can be found in [8]; this
284: concerns very special cases of matchings though, for which formulas for the Aπ can
285: be given. The general conjecture is still widely open.

417:                   3. Some conjectural properties of the polynomials
418:    In this section we study some properties of the polynomials Xπ (m). We present
419: precise conjectures about these polynomials, together with evidence for them. Note
420: that all conjectures concern both the FPL model (X = A) and the O(1) model
421: (X = Ψ). Thus each conjecture contains actually two of them, which become one
422: assuming the Razumov–Stragonov conjecture
423:    We will first give a combinatorial construction that is instrumental in the state-
424: ment of the conjectures; then we list conjectures about roots, special values, a very
425: special value, and coefficients of the polynomials Xπ (m).
426:

499: 3.2. Real roots. Now we can state our first conjecture, which gives a complete
500: description of all real roots of the polynomials Xπ (m):
501: Conjecture 3.2. All the real roots of the polynomials Xπ (m) are negative integers,
502: and −p appears with multiplicity mp (π). Equivalently, we have a factorization:

510: conjecture. We know indeed that Xπ (m) has degree d(π) in m, and that the degree
511: of Qπ (m) must be even since it is a real polynomial with no real roots. The following
512: proposition shows that the definition of mp (π) agrees with these constraints:

695: 3.5. Positivity of the coefficients. Our last conjecture is a bit different from the
696: other three ones, in that it does not deal with values of the polynomials, but their
697: coefficients:
698: Conjecture 3.10. For any π, the coefficients of Xπ (m) are non-negative.
699:    As always the data we have (up to |π| = 8 for Ψπ (m), and |π| = 5 for Aπ (m))
700: confirms this conjecture. Furthermore, it seems that the polynomials Qπ (m), whose
701: existence is predicted by Conjecture 3.2, also have nonnegative coefficients; this is
702: clearly stronger than Conjecture 3.10.
703:    We managed to compute the coefficient of degree d(π) − 1, and show that it is
704: indeed positive: see Theorem 4.1 in the next section. Note that by Theorem 2.1,
705: we know already that Xπ (m) is of degree d(π) with a positive leading coefficient,
706: so we will be interested in the subleading coefficient.
707: 
708:                   4. The subleading term of the polynomials
709:      In this section we will prove the following result:
710: Theorem 4.1. Given a matching π of size n, π 6= ()n , the coefficients of md(π)−1
711: in Aπ (m) and Ψπ (m) are equal. Moreover, the common value is positive.
712:    Note that not only this proves Conjecture 3.10 for a particular coefficient, it
713: gives a small new piece of evidence for the Razumov-Stroganov conjecture.
714:    We will first give a formula for this coefficient in the FPL case, and prove that
715: the result is positive; then we will compute the coefficient in the O(1) case, and
716: show that it coincides with the one in the FPL case. In a final section we will
717: deduce from the proof of Theorem 4.1 two formulas concerning products of hook
718: lengths, see Proposition 4.3.

724: removing a corner from this diagram, i.e. a cell of Y (π) which has no cell below it

1448: Ψα (τ ). The first one is given by (−1)d(β) τ d(β) as seen above, while the leading term

1450: which achieves the proof.                                                              
1451: 
1452:                                    7. Concluding remarks
1453:    Since our paper is centered around conjectures, the most immediate problem is
1454: to solve them. We listed four conjectures in Section 3, dealing both with the O(1)
1455: case and the FPL case, and then four extensions in Section 6.
1456:    The idea underlying these conjectures is that there should be a “combinatorial
1457: reciprocity theorem” ([19]) attached to these polynomials. That is, we believe there
1458: are interesting combinatorial objects depending on π such that Xπ (−p) is equal (up
1459: to sign) to the number of these objects with size p. The first two conjectures tell
1460: us in particular for which p such objects exist and that they should factorize for
1461: other values of p. It seems particularly important to guess what the numbers
1462: Gπ = Xπ (−|π|) count, as stressed in Section 3.4.
1463: 
1464:               POLYNOMIALS AND THE RAZUMOV–STROGANOV CONJECTURE                                                23
1465: 
1466: 
1467:     The conjectures themselves have interesting consequences: to take an example,
1468: proving Conjecture 3.2 for X = A gives some interesting relations for the numbers
1469: tπσ in Equation (3). And discovering what these numbers tπσ are may be a step in
1470: the direction of the Razumov–Stroganov conjecture, in the sense that it gives an
1471: expression for Aπ that could be compared to the expressions for Ψπ .

8:                                                   Abstract. We are interested in the enumeration of Fully Packed Loop config-
9:                                                   urations on a grid with a given noncrossing matching. By the recently proved
10:                                                   Razumov–Stroganov conjecture, these quantities also appear as groundstate
11:                                                   components in the Completely Packed Loop model.
12: 
13: 
14: 
15: 
16: arXiv:1002.4187v2 [math.CO] 21 Jun 2010
17:                                                      When considering matchings with p nested arches, these numbers are known
18:                                                   to be polynomials in p. In this article, we present several conjectures about
19:                                                   these polynomials: in particular, we describe all real roots, certain values
20:                                                   of these polynomials, and conjecture that the coefficients are positive. The
21:                                                   conjectures, which are of a combinatorial nature, are supported by strong
22:                                                   numerical evidence and the proofs of several special cases. We also give a
23:                                                   version of the conjectures when an extra parameter τ is added to the equations
24:                                                   defining the groundstate of the Completely Packed Loop model.
25: 
26: 
27: 
28: 
29:                                                                                Introduction
30:                                              The recently proved Razumov–Stroganov conjecture [23, 5] is a correspondence
31:                                           between, on the one hand, combinatorially defined quantities called Fully Packed
32:                                           Loop (FPL) configurations, and on the other hand, components of the groundstate
33:                                           vector of the Hamiltonian in the Completely Packed Loop model. These quantities
34:                                           are indexed by noncrossing, perfect matchings π of 2n points (cf. definition in
35:                                           Section 1.1).The number of FPL configurations with associated matching π will
36:                                           be denoted Aπ , while the corresponding components of the groundstate vector
37:                                           in the Completely Packed Loop model are written Ψπ . The Razumov–Stroganov
38:                                           conjecture states then that Aπ = Ψπ for any π.
39:                                              The goal of this article is to exhibit some surprising properties of these numbers
40:                                           when one studies matchings with nested arches (π)p = (· · · (π) · · · ), which means
41:                                           that there are p nested arches above the matching π. It was conjectured in [33], and
42:                                           subsequently proved in [7, 14], that the quantities A(π)p and Ψ(π)p are polynomial
43:                                           in p. We define then the polynomial Aπ (t) such that Aπ (p) = A(π)p when p is a
44:                                           nonnegative integer.
45: 
46:                                              This paper deals with certain conjectures about these polynomials. Let π be
47:                                           a matching with n arches: the main conjectures deal with the description of real
48:                                           roots of the polynomials (Conjecture 3.5), their values at negative integers between
49:                                           1 − n and −1 (Conjecture 3.8), evaluations at −n (Conjecture 3.11) and finally the
50:                                           positivity of the coefficients (Conjecture 3.12). We gather some evidence for the
51:                                           conjectures, and prove some special cases (cf. Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 5.1). In
52:                                           the Completely Packed Loop model, one can in fact define bivariate polynomials
53:                                           Ψ(τ, t) that coincide with Ψ(t) at τ = 1; it turns out that most of our conjectures
54:                                           admit a natural generalization in this context also, which in some sense is more
55:                                           evidence for the original conjectures.
56:                                              We believe these conjectures can help us understand better the numbers Aπ .
57:                                           Moreover, our work on these conjectures has some interesting byproducts: first,
58:                                                                                         1
59: 
60: 2                         TIAGO FONSECA AND PHILIPPE NADEAU
61: 
62: 
63: the conjectured root multiplicities of the polynomials Aπ (t) have nice combina-
64: torial descriptions in terms of π (see Section 3.1). Then, from the proof of Theo-
65: rem 4.1, we deduce some nice formulas about products of hook lengths of partitions
66: (Proposition 4.3). Also, the proof of Theorem 5.1 involves the introduction of a
67: new multivariate integral.
68:    Let us give a detailed outline of this article, where π will refer to a matching
69: with n arches. In Section 1, we define the quantities Aπ and Ψπ , and formulate the
70: Razumov–Stroganov conjecture. We introduce in Section 2 the central objects of
71: our study, the polynomials Aπ (t). It is also recalled how to approach the compu-
72: tation of these polynomials.
73:    The main conjectures about the Aπ (t) are gathered in Section 3: they are Conjec-
74: tures 3.5, 3.8, 3.11 and 3.12. We give also numerous evidence for these conjectures,
75: the most important one being perhaps that they have been checked for all matchings
76: with n ≤ 8.
77:    The next two sections address particular cases of some of the conjectures: in
78: Section 4, we are concerned with the computation of the subleading term of the
79: polynomials. The main result, Theorem 4.1, shows that this is a positive number
80: both for Aπ (t); it is thus a special case of Conjecture 3.12. We give two proofs of this
81: result, from which we derive some nice formulas mixing hook lengths and contents
82: of partitions (Proposition 4.3). Section 5 is concerned with the proof that if {1, 2n}
83: is not an arch in π, then Aπ (−1) = 0; this is a special case of Conjecture 3.5. The
84: proof relies on the multivariate polynomial extension of Ψπ , the main properties of
85: which are recalled briefly.
86:    Section 6 deals with certain bivariate polynomials Ψπ (τ, t) which specialize to
87: Aπ (t) when τ = 1. It turns out that the conjectures of Section 3 generalize in a
88: very satisfying way. We finally give two appendices: Appendix A gives a proof
89: of the technical result in Theorem 3.1, while Appendix B lists some data on the
90: polynomials Aπ (t).

93:    We first introduce matchings and different notions related to them. We then
94: describe Fully Packed Loop configurations, as well as the Completely Packed Loop
95: model.

264: interest, mainly because of the Razumov–Stroganov (ex-)conjecture.

287:             2. Matchings with nested arches and polynomials
288: 2.1. Definitions and results. In [33], Zuber computed some Ψ(π)p for some small
289: matchings π, and p = 0, 1, 2, .... Among other things, he conjectured the following:
290: Theorem 2.1 ([7, 14]). For any matching π and p a nonnegative integer, the
291: quantity A(π)p can be written in the following form:
292:                                              Pπ (p)
293:                                    A(π)p =          ,
294:                                              d(π)!
295: where Pπ (p) is a polynomial in p of degree d(π) with integer coefficients, and leading
296: coefficient equal to d(π)!/Hπ .
297:    This was proved first by Caselli, Krattenthaler, Lass and Nadeau in [7] for A(π)p ,
298: and by Fonseca and Zinn-Justin in [14] for Ψ(π)p . Because of this polynomiality
299: property, we introduce the following notations :
300: Definition 2.2 (Aπ (t) and Ψπ (t)). We let Aπ (t) (respectively Ψπ (t)) be the poly-
301: nomial in t such that Aπ (p) = A(π)p (resp. Ψπ (p) = Ψ(π)p ) for all integers p ≥ 0.
302:     By the Razumov–Stroganov conjecture 1.5 one has clearly for all π:
303:                                    Aπ (t) = Ψπ (t).
304: We introduced two different notations so that the origin of the quantities involved
305: becomes clearer; in most of this paper however we will only use the notation Aπ (t).
306: It is the objective of this paper to investigate these polynomials, and give evidence
307: that they possess very interesting properties, in particular when they are evaluated
308: at negative integers. The following proposition sums up some properties of the
309: polynomials.
310: Proposition 2.3. The polynomial Aπ (t) has degree d(π) and leading coefficient
311: 1/Hπ . Furthermore, we have Aπ (t) = Aπ∗ (t), and A(π)` (t) = Aπ (t + `) for any
312: nonnegative integer `.
313:   The first part comes from Theorem 2.1, while the rest is clear when t is a non-
333: not larger than N : this is the hook content formula, cf. [26] for instance.
334:    Equation (3) above can be derived from [27, Equation (4)] (itself based on the
335: work [7]) together with Conjecture 3.4 in the same paper: this conjecture and the
336: derivation are proved in [21].

420:                                            3. The main conjectures
421:    In this section we present several conjectures about the polynomials Aπ (t). For
422: each of them, we will give strong supporting evidence. We will first give a combi-
423: natorial construction that is essential in the statement of the conjectures.
424: 3.1. Combinatorics. We give two rules which define certain integers attached to
425: a matching π. It turns out that the two rules are equivalent, which is the content
426: of Theorem 3.1.
427:    Let π be a link pattern, and n = |π| its number of arches. We let Y (π), d(π)
428: be the Young diagram of π and its number of boxes respectively, as defined in
429: Section 1.1. We also use the notation x
430:                                       b = 2n + 1 − x for x ∈ [[1, 2n]].
431:    Rule A: For p between 1 and n − 1, we consider the set AL    p (π) of arches {a1 , a2 }
432: such that a1 ≤ p and p < a2 < pb, and the set AR    p (π) of arches {a1 , a2 } such that
433:                                                          R
434: p < a1 < pb and pb ≤ a2 . It is clear that |AL
522:    We will use this result in Section 3.2.1.
523: 
524: 3.2. The conjectures. The rest of this section will consist of the statement of
525: Conjectures 3.5, 3.8, 3.11 and 3.12, together with evidence in their support. The
526: first three conjectures are related to values of the polynomials Aπ (t) when the argu-
527: ment t is a negative integer; what these conjectures imply is that some mysterious
528: combinatorics occur around these values Aπ (−p). The fourth conjecture states
529: simply that the polynomials Aπ (t) have positive coefficients, and is thus slightly
530: different in spirit than the other ones, though they are clearly related.
531:     The principal evidence in support of the conjectures, as well as the source of
532: their discovery, is the following result:
533: 
534: Fact 3.4. Conjectures 3.5, 3.8 and 3.12 are true for all matchings π such that
535: π ≤ 8. Conjecture 3.11 is true for all n ≤ 8.
536: 
537:    The corresponding polynomials Aπ (t) were indeed computed in Mathematica for
538: these values of π thanks to Formula 6, and each conjecture was then checked from
539: these exact expressions; note that there are 1430 matchings |π| such that |π| = 8.
540: In Appendix B we list the polynomials Aπ (t) for |π| = 4.
541:

545: 3.2.1. Real roots. The first conjecture gives a complete description of all real roots
546: of the polynomials Aπ (t):
547: 
548: Conjecture 3.5. All the real roots of the polynomials Aπ (t) are negative integers,
549: and −p appears with multiplicity mp (π). Equivalently, we have a factorization:
550:                                                         
551:                                      |π|−1
552:                              1        Y
553:                  Aπ (t) =         ·       (t + p)mp (π)  · Qπ (t),
554:                           |d(π)|!     p=1
555: 
556: 
557: where Qπ (t) is a polynomial with integer coefficients and no real roots.
558: 
559:    We must verify first that the definition of the multiplicities is coherent with
560: this conjecture. We know indeed by Theorem 2.1 that Aπ (t) has degree d(π) in t;
561: furthermore the degree of Qπ (t) is necessarily even, since it is a real polynomial with
562: no real roots. This means that the sum of the mp (π) should not be larger than d(π),
563: and should be of the same parity: this is precisely the content of Proposition 3.3.
564:    It is also immediately checked that the conjecture is compatible with the two
565: stability properties from Proposition 2.3, that is Aπ (t) = Aπ∗ (t) and A(π)` (t) =
566: Aπ (t + `) for any nonnegative integer `. Indeed mp (π) = mp (π ∗ ) is immediately
567: seen from either one of the rules, as is mp+` ((π)` ) = mp (π).
568:

728: 
729: 3.2.4. Positivity of the coefficients. Our last conjecture is a bit different from the
730: other three ones, in that it does not deal with values of the polynomials, but their
731: coefficients:
732: Conjecture 3.12. For any π, the coefficients of Aπ (t) are nonnegative.
733:    It seems in fact to be true that the polynomials Qπ (t) –whose existence is pre-
734: dicted by Conjecture 3.5– also only have nonnegative coefficients.
735:    By Theorem 2.1, we know already that Aπ (t) is of degree d(π) with a positive
736: leading coefficient, so we will be interested in the subleading coefficient, that is, the
737: coefficient of td(π)−1 . We managed to compute this coefficient and prove that it is
738: indeed positive: this is Theorem 4.1 in the next section.
739: 
740:                  4. The subleading term of the polynomials
741:   In this section we will prove the following result:
742: Theorem 4.1. Given a matching π of size n, π 6= ()n , the coefficient of td(π)−1 in
743: Aπ (t) is positive.
744:    This is a special case of Conjecture 3.12. We will give two proofs of this theorem,
745: one starting from the expression (3), the other based on the expression (8). As a
746: byproduct of these proofs, we will deduce two formulas concerning products of hook
747: lengths (Proposition 4.3).
748: 4.1. First proof. We use first the expression of Aπ (t) given by the sum in Equa-
749: tion (3):                         X

751:                                         σ≤π
752: 
753:    We need to gather the terms contributing to the coefficient of td(π)−1 : they are
754: of two kinds, depending on whether Sσ (t + 1 − n) has degree d(σ) equal to d(π) or
755: d(π) − 1. Since σ ≤ π, the first case occurs only for σ = π, while the second case

1008: Corollary 5.2. If m1 (π) = 1, then (t + 1) divides the polynomial Aπ (t).
1009:    Indeed m1 (π) = 1 precisely when there is no arch between 1 and 2n in π (cf.
1010: Rule A in Section 3.1), which means that π cannot be written as (π 0 ). For the same

1415: 
1416:                                  7. Further questions
1417: 7.1. Solving the conjectures. Since our paper is centered around conjectures,
1418: the most immediate problem is to solve them. We listed four conjectures in Section 3
1419: which concern roots, specializations and coefficients of the polynomials Aπ (t). The
1420: difficulty here is that existing expressions for the polynomials Aπ (t), namely (3)
1421: and (8), consist of certain sums of polynomials, so that it makes it uneasy to
1422: find real roots of Aπ (t), and more generally the sign variations when t is a real
1423: variable. For the same reasons, it is hard to figure out where the factorization
1424: from Conjecture 3.8 comes from. Furthermore, both expressions (3) and (8) involve
1425: negative signs, so that the positivity of coefficients is not at all obvious. One way to
1426: attack the conjectures would be then to find new expressions for the polynomials;
1427: this could be done by either understanding better the quantities involved in (3)
1428: and (8), or coming up with a new decomposition of the FPLs counted by A(π)p for
1429: instance.
1430:    Note also that the linear relations from Definition 1.3, which determine the
1431: Aπ by the Razumov Stroganov correspondence 1.5, do not seem to be helpful in
1432: the case of nested arches. Indeed given a matching (π)p , then the linear relation
1433: 
1434:                    POLYNOMIALS COUNTING FPL CONFIGURATIONS                            23
1435: 
1436: 
1437: corresponding to A(π)p involves not only quantities of the form A(π0 )p or A(π0 )p−1 ,
1440: enough (cf. [7, Theorem 6.7]), and these ones are “stable” with respect to these
1441: Razumov–Stroganov linear relations: it would be very interesting to study these
1442: more general polynomials and find out how our conjectures can be extended.
1443:    Another angle to attack some of the conjectures (namely Conj. 3.5, 3.8 and
1444: their τ counterparts 6.1, 6.3) would be to extend the approach used in the proof
1445: of Theorem 5.1: one first needs to extend the multivariate integral definition (24)
1446: to any integer p, which can easily be done. The problem is that the expressions
1447: obtained are fairly more complicated and intricate than in the case p = −1. This
1448: is work in progress.
1449: 
1450: 
1451: 
1452: 7.2. Combinatorial reciprocity. The idea underlying our conjectures (Conjec-
1453: ture 3.12 excepted) is that there should be a “combinatorial reciprocity theorem”
1454: ([25]) attached to these polynomials. That is, we believe there exist yet-to-be-
1455: discovered combinatorial objects depending on π such that Aπ (−p) is equal (up to
1456: sign) to the number of these objects with size p. The most well-known example
1457: in the literature of such a phenomenon concerns the Ehrhart polynomial iP (t) of
1458: a lattice polytope P , which counts the number of lattice points in tP when t is a
1459: positive integer: for such t, Ehrhart reciprocity then tells us that (−1)dim P iP (−t)
1460: counts lattice points strictly in tP (see [1] for instance).
1461:    It is natural to wonder if our problem fits in the domain of Ehrhart theory, since
1462: most known examples of combinatorial reciprocity can be formulated in terms of
1463: Ehrhart polynomials: see for instance certain polynomials attached to graphs [3, 4].
1468: ()()()() = ()4 for instance, one computes from the explicit expression given in
1469: Appendix B that Aπ (−2) = −1 while Aπ (−4) = 9. Moreover, one can also show
1470: that if Conjecture 3.5 holds, then given any finite set S of negative integers (included
1471: say in {−1, . . . , 1 − n}) there exists a matching π of size n such that the set of
1472: negative roots of Aπ (t) is precisely S. This is clearly a behaviour contrasting with
1473: Ehrhart polynomials, and even their generalizations to inside-out polytopes [2].
1474:    Conjectures 3.5 and 3.8 tell us in particular for which values of p objects counted
1475: by |Aπ (−p)| should exist, and moreover that such objects should split for certain
1476: values of p. As pointed out in Section 3.2.3, Conjectures 3.8 and 3.11 make it
1477: particularly important to figure out what the numbers Gπ = Aπ (−|π|) count.
1478: 
1479: 
1480: 
1481: 7.3. Consequences of the conjectures. The conjectures have interesting con-
1482: sequences regarding the numbers aπσ involved in Equation (3), since for instance
1483: Conjecture 3.5 directly implies certain linear relations among these numbers. Dis-
1484: covering what these numbers aπσ are is a step in the direction of a new proof of
1485: the Razumov–Stroganov conjecture, in the sense that it gives an expression for Aπ
1486: that could be compared to the expressions for Ψπ . We note also that a conjec-
1487: tural expression for these numbers aπσ was given in [30], which if true would in
1488: fact give another proof of the Razumov–Stroganov conjecture; a special case of this
1489: expression is proven in [22].

15:                                                   group L is graph-restrictive if there exists a constant c(L) such that, for every locally-L
16:                                                   pair (Γ, G) and an arc (u, v) of Γ, the inequality |Guv | ≤ c(L) holds.
17:                                                       Using this terminology, the Weiss Conjecture says that primitive groups are graph-
18:                                                   restrictive. We propose a very strong generalisation of this conjecture: a group is graph-
19:                                                   restrictive if and only if it is semiprimitive. (A transitive permutation group is said to
20:                                                   be semiprimitive if each of its normal subgroups is either transitive or semiregular.) Our
21:                                                   main result is a proof of one of the two implications of this conjecture, namely that graph-
22:                                                   restrictive groups are semiprimitive. We also collect the known results and prove some
23:                                                   new ones regarding the other implication.
24: 
25: 
26: 
27: 
28:                                                                                     1. Introduction
29:                                              Unless explicitly stated, all graphs considered in this paper are finite and simple. A
30:                                           graph Γ is said to be G-vertex-transitive if G is a subgroup of Aut(Γ) acting transitively on
31:                                           the vertex-set VΓ of Γ. Similarly, Γ is said to be G-arc-transitive if G acts transitively on
32:                                           the arcs of Γ (that is, on the ordered pairs of adjacent vertices of Γ). When G = Aut(Γ),
50:                                              2000 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 20B25; Secondary 05E18.
51:                                              Key words and phrases. arc-transitive graphs, semiprimitive group, primitive group, graph-restrictive
52:                                           group, Weiss Conjecture.
53:                                              The second author is supported by UWA as part of the Australian Council Federation Fellowship Project
54:                                           FF0776186. The second author would also like to thank the Institute of Mathematics, Physics and Mechanics
55:                                           at the University of Ljubljana for the warm hospitality.

58: 2                          P. POTOČNIK, P. SPIGA, AND G. VERRET
59: 
60: 
61: said to be locally-L. More generally, if P is a permutation group property, then (Γ, G) will
62:                                     Γ(v)
63: be called locally-P provided that Gv     possesses the property P.
73: its natural action on 3 points is graph-restrictive and the constant c(Sym(3)) can be chosen
74: to be 16. Several authors generalised Tutte’s result from Sym(3) to other primitive groups,
75: which eventually led Weiss to pose the following conjecture.
76: Weiss Conjecture [29, Conjecture 3.12]. Primitive groups are graph-restrictive.
77: 
78:    While much effort has been deployed in the attempts to prove the Weiss Conjecture, very
79: few authors considered graph-restrictiveness of imprimitive groups. We will show that the
80: main results which have been used to attack the Weiss Conjecture can be generalised from
81: primitive groups to semiprimitive groups, which we conjecture are graph-restrictive. (A
82: transitive permutation group is said to be semiprimitive if each of its normal subgroups is
83: either transitive or semiregular. Contrary to [2], we consider regular groups to be semiprim-
84: itive). In fact, encouraged by some results proved in this paper, we conjecture the following
85: characterisation of graph-restrictive groups.
86: Conjecture 3. A permutation group is graph-restrictive if and only if it is semiprimitive.
87:    Our goal in this paper is to collect the known results regarding this conjecture and to
88: establish new ones. One of our main results is the following theorem (proved in Section 4),
89: which proves one of the two implications of the above conjecture.
90: Theorem 4. Every graph-restrictive group is semiprimitive.
91:   The structure of the paper is very simple. Section 2 is a summary of all important results,
92: with each new theorem getting its own section later for details and proofs.
93: 
94:                                        2. Summary
95:    We start with a review of previously known results about graph-restrictiveness of per-
96: mutation groups. It is a rather trivial observation that regular permutation groups L are
97: graph-restrictive, with c(L) = 1. One of the earliest non-trivial results with important ap-
98: plications towards the Weiss Conjecture is Theorem 5, a variant of the Thompson-Wielandt
99:                                                                                           [1]
100: theorem (see [24]). Given a G-arc-transitive graph Γ and (u, v) an arc of Γ, denote by Guv
101: the subgroup of G fixing Γ(u) and Γ(v) point-wise.
102: Theorem 5. [9, Corollary 2.3] Let (Γ, G) be a locally-primitive pair and let (u, v) be an arc
103:              [1]
104: of Γ. Then, Guv is a p-group for some prime p.
105: 
106:                        ON GRAPH-RESTRICTIVE PERMUTATION GROUPS                                  3
107: 
108: 
109:    Theorem 5 imposes a very strong restriction on the structure of Guv . For example, if
110: Γ has valency d, then Guv contains a normal p-subgroup of index at most (d − 1)!2 . As
111: we will see, Theorem 5 was the first step in the proof of many results regarding the Weiss
112: Conjecture.
113:    Despite considerable effort by many authors, the Weiss conjecture is still open. Some
114: important subcases have however been dealt with. For example, the case of primitive groups
115: of affine type is almost complete (see [26, Theorem]). (A primitive group L is said to be
116: of affine type if it contains a regular abelian normal subgroup Q. In particular, as L is
117: primitive, Q is an elementary abelian q-group, for some prime q, and L has degree a power
118: of q.)
119:    Note that [26, Theorem] was misquoted in [6, Theorem 3.1] where the authors concluded
120: that all primitive groups of affine type are graph-restrictive, which does not follow from [26,
121: Theorem]. This has unfortunate consequences. For example, they claim that primitive
122: groups of degree at most 20 are graph-restrictive [6, Proposition 4.1], but their proof relies
123: on [6, Theorem 3.1] in an essential way. In fact, to the best of our knowledge, it is still
124: unknown whether the primitive group of affine type Sym(3) wr Sym(2) with its product
126: of groups of small degree in Section 3.
127:    The proof [26, Theorem] depends heavily on Theorem 5. Another important case of the
128: Weiss Conjecture where Theorem 5 plays a crucial role is the 2-transitive case, which has
129: been settled as the culmination of work by Weiss and Trofimov.
130: Theorem 6. 2-transitive groups are graph-restrictive.
131:    The proof of Theorem 6 is scattered over many papers and hence this result is somewhat
132: part of folklore. In Section 6, we try to remedy this situation by collecting the various parts
133: of the proofs and giving a brief overview of the argument.
134:    Note that, by Burnside’s Theorem [7, Theorem 3.5B], transitive groups of prime degree
135: are either 2-transitive or of affine type. Together with Tutte’s result, [26, Theorem] and
136: Theorem 6 imply that transitive groups of prime degree are graph-restrictive
137:    Little was previously known about graph-restrictiveness of imprimitive groups. Recently,
138: Sami [15] has shown that dihedral groups of odd degree are graph-restrictive, generalising
139: Tutte’s theorem (as Sym(3) ∼   = D3 ), and the third author generalised this result to the wider
146: permutation group is called quasiprimitive if each of its non-trivial normal subgroups is
147: transitive. It is called biquasiprimitive if it is not quasiprimitive and each of its non-trivial
148: normal subgroups has at most two orbits.) This lead her to conjecture that quasiprimitive
149: groups are graph-restrictive, a conjecture stronger than the Weiss Conjecture but weaker
150: than Conjecture 3.
151:    Let us now discuss our new results supporting the conjecture that semiprimitive groups
152: are graph-restrictive. It turns out that many of the important tools that are available for
153: primitive groups are also available for semiprimitive groups. For example, the starting point
154: 
155: 4                           P. POTOČNIK, P. SPIGA, AND G. VERRET
156: 
157: 
158: for most of the results in the primitive case is Theorem 5, which was recently generalised
159: to the semiprimitive case by the second author.
160: Theorem 7. [16, Corollary 3] Let (Γ, G) be a locally-semiprimitive pair and let (u, v) be
161:                     [1]
162: an arc of Γ. Then, Guv is a p-group for some prime p.
185:   In this section, we give a few examples of how Corollary 9 and Corollary 10 can be
186: combined to show that certain semiprimitive permutation groups are graph-restrictive and
187: examine the status of Conjecture 3 for groups of small degree.
188: Example 11. Let n ≥ 3 be odd and let L = Dn be the dihedral group of order 2n in its
189: natural action on n points. Then |Lx | = 2 and, if l is a generator of the cyclic subgroup
190: Cn ≤ L, then hLx , Llx i = L. By Corollary 10, it follows that L is graph-restrictive (in fact,
191: we can take c(L) = 16).
192: Example 12. Let p be a prime. Denote by Z the centre of GL(2, p), that is, the subgroup
193: of GL(2, p) consisting of the diagonal matrices. Let G be a subgroup of GL(2, p) with
194: SL(2, p) ≤ G, and let K be a subgroup of G ∩ Z. Clearly, GL(2, p) acts as a group of
195: automorphisms on the 2-dimensional vector space of row vectors F2p . We let Ω denote the
196: set of orbits of K on F2p \ {0}. Since K E G, the group G acts on Ω and, by a direct
197: computation, we see that K is the kernel of the action of G on Ω. Write L = G/K for the
198: permutation group induced by G on Ω.
233:     (3) Sym(5) of degree 10 acting on the unordered pairs of a 5-set, or
234:     (4) Sym(4) of degree 12 acting on the ordered pairs of a 4-set.
235:    In particular, Conjecture 3 is true for permutation groups of degree at most 8. As we
236: already noted, it is unknown whether the primitive group Sym(3) wr Sym(2) of degree 9
237: is graph-restrictive, hence even the Weiss Conjecture is not known to hold for groups of
238: degree 9 or more.
239: 
240:                                  4. Proof of Theorem 4
241:    The proof of Theorem 4 relies on a construction inspired by the wreath extension (see [11,
242: Section 8.1]), which has been used to solve some instances of the Embedding Galois Problem
243: (see [8, Chapter 3]). We start by establishing some notation and some preliminary lemmas
244: that will be necessary in the proof of Theorem 4.
245:    Let L be a finite permutation group on a set Λ and let K be an intransitive normal
246: subgroup
247: T          of L. Denote by ∆ the set of orbits of K on its action on Λ. Replacing K by
248:   λ∈Λ (KLλ ), we may assume that K is the kernel of the action of L on ∆. We let S denote

15:                                                  group L is graph-restrictive if there exists a constant c(L) such that, for every locally-L
16:                                                  pair (Γ, G) and an arc (u, v) of Γ, the inequality |Guv | ≤ c(L) holds.
17:                                                      Using this terminology, the Weiss Conjecture says that primitive groups are graph-
18:                                                  restrictive. We propose a very strong generalisation of this conjecture: a group is graph-
19:                                                  restrictive if and only if it is semiprimitive. (A transitive permutation group is said to
20:                                                  be semiprimitive if each of its normal subgroups is either transitive or semiregular.) Our
21:                                                  main result is a proof of one of the two implications of this conjecture, namely that graph-
22:                                                  restrictive groups are semiprimitive. We also collect the known results and prove some
23:                                                  new ones regarding the other implication.

28:                                                                                    1. Introduction
29:                                             Unless explicitly stated, all graphs considered in this paper are finite and simple. A
30:                                          graph Γ is said to be G-vertex-transitive if G is a subgroup of Aut(Γ) acting transitively on
31:                                          the vertex-set VΓ of Γ. Similarly, Γ is said to be G-arc-transitive if G acts transitively on
32:                                          the arcs of Γ (that is, on the ordered pairs of adjacent vertices of Γ). When G = Aut(Γ),
50:                                             2000 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 20B25; Secondary 05E18.
51:                                             Key words and phrases. arc-transitive graphs, semiprimitive group, primitive group, graph-restrictive
52:                                          group, Weiss Conjecture.
53:                                             The second author is supported by UWA as part of the Australian Council Federation Fellowship Project
54:                                          FF0776186. The second author would also like to thank the Institute of Mathematics, Physics and Mechanics
55:                                          at the University of Ljubljana for the warm hospitality.

59: 
60: 
61: said to be locally-L. More generally, if P is a permutation group property, then (Γ, G) will
62:                                     Γ(v)
63: be called locally-P provided that Gv     possesses the property P.
73: its natural action on 3 points is graph-restrictive and the constant c(Sym(3)) can be chosen
74: to be 16. Several authors generalised Tutte’s result from Sym(3) to other primitive groups,
75: which eventually led Weiss to pose the following conjecture.
76: Weiss Conjecture [32, Conjecture 3.12]. Primitive groups are graph-restrictive.
77: 
78:    While much effort has been deployed in the attempts to prove the Weiss Conjecture, very
79: few authors considered graph-restrictiveness of imprimitive groups. We will show that the
80: main results which have been used to attack the Weiss Conjecture can be generalised from
81: primitive groups to semiprimitive groups, which we conjecture are graph-restrictive. (A
82: transitive permutation group is said to be semiprimitive if each of its normal subgroups is
83: either transitive or semiregular. Contrary to [2], we consider regular groups to be semiprim-
84: itive). In fact, encouraged by some results proved in this paper, we conjecture the following
85: characterisation of graph-restrictive groups.
86: Conjecture 3. A permutation group is graph-restrictive if and only if it is semiprimitive.
87:    Our goal in this paper is to collect the known results regarding this conjecture and to
88: establish new ones. One of our main results is the following theorem (proved in Section 4),
89: which proves one of the two implications of the above conjecture.
90: Theorem 4. Every graph-restrictive group is semiprimitive.
91:   The structure of the paper is very simple. Section 2 is a summary of all important results,
92: with each new theorem getting its own section later for details and proofs.
93: 
94:                                        2. Summary
95:    We start with a review of previously known results about graph-restrictiveness of per-
96: mutation groups. It is a rather trivial observation that regular permutation groups L are
97: graph-restrictive, with c(L) = 1. One of the earliest non-trivial results with important ap-
98: plications towards the Weiss Conjecture is Theorem 5, a variant of the Thompson-Wielandt
99:                                                                                           [1]
100: theorem (see [27]). Given a G-arc-transitive graph Γ and (u, v) an arc of Γ, denote by Guv
101: the subgroup of G fixing Γ(u) and Γ(v) point-wise.
102: Theorem 5. [9, Corollary 2.3] Let (Γ, G) be a locally-primitive pair and let (u, v) be an arc
103:              [1]
104: of Γ. Then, Guv is a p-group for some prime p.
105: 
106:                        ON GRAPH-RESTRICTIVE PERMUTATION GROUPS                                  3
107: 
108: 
109:   Theorem 5 imposes a very strong restriction on the structure of Guv . For example, if
110: Γ has valency d, then Guv contains a normal p-subgroup of index at most (d − 1)!2 . As
111: we will see, Theorem 5 was the first step in the proof of many results regarding the Weiss
112: Conjecture.
113:   Despite considerable effort by many authors, the Weiss Conjecture is still open. Some
114: important subcases have however been dealt with, the most important being the 2-transitive
115: case, which has been settled as the culmination of work by Trofimov and Weiss.
116: 
117: Theorem 6. 2-transitive groups are graph-restrictive.
118: 
119:    The proof of Theorem 6 is scattered over many papers and hence this result is somewhat
120: part of folklore. In Section 6, we try to remedy this situation by collecting the various parts
121: of the proofs and giving a brief overview of the argument.
122:    Another case of the Weiss Conjecture which was completed very recently is the case of
123: primitive groups of so-called linear type. Let q be aprime-power and let V be a vector space
124:                                                        V
125: over Fq of dimension n. For m ≤ n, denote by m            the set of all m-dimensional subspaces
126:                           V
127: of V and               [   ]
128:         by PSL(n, q)         the projective special linear group PSL(n, q) in its natural action
129:                          m
130:      V
131: on m    . A primitive group L is said to be of linear type if it contains a normal subgroup
132:                             V
133: isomorphic to PSL(n, q)[m] . In [22, 23, 24, 33], it was proved that primitive groups of linear
134: type are graph-restrictive.
135:    Another important case of the Weiss Conjecture is the case of primitive groups of affine
136: type, which is almost complete (see [29, Theorem]). (A primitive group L is said to be
137: of affine type if it contains a regular abelian normal subgroup Q. In particular, as L is
138: primitive, Q is an elementary abelian q-group, for some prime q, and L has degree a power
139: of q.)
140:    By Burnside’s Theorem [7, Theorem 3.5B], transitive groups of prime degree are either
141: 2-transitive or of affine type. Together with Tutte’s result, [29, Theorem] and Theorem 6
142: imply that transitive groups of prime degree are graph-restrictive.
143:    Note that some of the above results were misquoted in [6]. For example, [29, Theorem]
144: was misquoted in [6, Theorem 3.1] where the authors concluded that all primitive groups
145: of affine type are graph-restrictive, which does not follow from [29, Theorem]. This has
146: unfortunate consequences. For example, they claim that primitive groups of degree at most
169: permutation group is called quasiprimitive if each of its non-trivial normal subgroups is
170: transitive. It is called biquasiprimitive if it is not quasiprimitive and each of its non-trivial
171: normal subgroups has at most two orbits.) This lead her to conjecture that quasiprimitive
172: groups are graph-restrictive, a conjecture stronger than the Weiss Conjecture but weaker
173: than Conjecture 3.
174:    Let us now discuss our new results supporting the conjecture that semiprimitive groups
175: are graph-restrictive. It turns out that many of the important tools that are available for
176: primitive groups are also available for semiprimitive groups. For example, the starting point
177: for most of the results in the primitive case is Theorem 5, which was recently generalised
178: to the semiprimitive case by the second author.
179: Theorem 7. [16, Corollary 3] Let (Γ, G) be a locally-semiprimitive pair and let (u, v) be
180:                     [1]
181: an arc of Γ. Then, Guv is a p-group for some prime p.
182:    Theorem 7 is very useful to prove that certain semiprimitive permutation groups are
183: graph-restrictive. We will give some examples of this, but first we must introduce the
184: following more general version of Definition 2.
185: Definition 8. Let p be a prime. A transitive permutation group L is p-graph-restrictive
204:   In this section, we give a few examples of how Corollary 9 and Corollary 10 can be
205: combined to show that certain semiprimitive permutation groups are graph-restrictive and
206: examine the status of Conjecture 3 for groups of small degree.
207: 
208:                        ON GRAPH-RESTRICTIVE PERMUTATION GROUPS                                5
209: 
210: 
211: Example 11. Let n ≥ 3 be odd and let L = Dn be the dihedral group of order 2n in its
212: natural action on n points. Then |Lx | = 2 and, if l is a generator of the cyclic subgroup
213: Cn ≤ L, then hLx , Llx i = L. By Corollary 10, it follows that L is graph-restrictive (in fact,
214: we can take c(L) = 16).
215: Example 12. Let p be a prime. Denote by Z the centre of GL(2, p), that is, the subgroup of
216: GL(2, p) consisting of the scalar matrices. Let G be a subgroup of GL(2, p) with SL(2, p) ≤
217: G, and let K be a subgroup of G ∩ Z. Clearly, GL(2, p) acts as a group of automorphisms
261: 
262: 
263:    In particular, Conjecture 3 is true for permutation groups of degree at most 8. As we
264: already noted, it is unknown whether the primitive group Sym(3) wr Sym(2) of degree 9
265: is graph-restrictive, hence even the Weiss Conjecture is not known to hold for groups of
266: degree 9 or more.
