268:                                       4. Proof of Theorem 4
269:    The proof of Theorem 4 relies on a construction inspired by the wreath extension (see [11,
270: Section 8.1]), which has been used to solve some instances of the Embedding Galois Problem
271: (see [8, Chapter 3]). We start by establishing some notation and some preliminary lemmas
272: that will be necessary in the proof of Theorem 4.
273:    Let L be a finite permutation group on a set Λ and let K be an intransitive normal
274: subgroup
275: T          of L. Denote by ∆ the set of orbits of K on its action on Λ. Replacing K by
276:   λ∈Λ (KLλ ), we may assume that K is the kernel of the action of L on ∆. We let S denote

130:       COMPUTATIONAL APPROACHES TO CONSECUTIVE PATTERN AVOIDANCE IN PERMUTATIONS                                 3
131: 
132: 
133:                        2. Consecutive Pattern Avoidance via Clusters
134:    Let B be a set of fixed patterns that we would like to avoid (consecutively). We may assume
135: that B contains no redundancies (i.e., there does not exist p1 , p2 ∈ B with p1 6= p2 such that p1
136: contains p2 ). Again, α(n) will be the number of length n permutations avoiding B.
623: 
624:                             4. Asymptotic Approximations Using CAV
625:    Let B = {p} be a set containing a single pattern. In [11], Warlimont gave a conjecture on the
626: asymptotics of α(n):
627: 
628: (17)                                        α(n) ∼ γ · ρn · n!
629: 
630: 
631: 
632: where γ and ρ are constants depending only on the single pattern p. Some initial asymptotic
633: results for α(n) were proven by Elizalde in [4]. Recently, Ehrenborg, Kitaev, and Perry prove this
634: conjecture in [2]. With this result established, we can compute approximate values of γ and ρ for
635: various single patterns.
636: 
637:     COMPUTATIONAL APPROACHES TO CONSECUTIVE PATTERN AVOIDANCE IN PERMUTATIONS                        11
638: 
639: 
640:    Elizalde and Noy gave some approximations of γ and ρ for length 3 and a few length 4 pat-
641: terns in [5]. Aldred, Atkinson, and McCaughan also gave approximations for the ρ values of
642: the single length 4 patterns. Using the Maple package CAV, we can empirically verify these ap-
643: proximations and also quickly produce many new approximations. For example, the procedure
644: AsymApprox(p,N,d) will give approximate values (up to d decimal digits) for γ and ρ for the
645: pattern p by computing α(N − 2), α(N − 1), and α(N ) and computing their ratios. For example,
677: 
678:    Despite this, there is a lot of room for improvement algorithmically and quite a few new open
679: problems/conjectures arise. Some of the conjectures are listed below.
680: 
681: Elizalde and Noy provided the following conjecture in [5]:
682: Conjecture 1. For a fixed pattern length k, the increasing pattern σ = 12 . . . k is the “maximal”
683: pattern, in the sense that ασ (n) ≥ αp (n) for all p ∈ Sk and all n.
684: 
685:

688: pattern, in the sense that αp (n) ≥ ασ (n) for all p ∈ Sk and all n.
689: Conjecture 3. For a fixed pattern length k, the pattern set B = {12 . . . k, 23 . . . k1} is the “maxi-
690: mal” pattern set among sets of 2 patterns, in the sense that αB (n) ≥ αB ′ (n) for all B ′ ∈ S2k and

543: 5     Concluding remarks
544: Our interest in developing a version of the nonvanishing theorem for multisets has grown out
545: of an attempt to prove Snevily’s conjecture [14] in a particular case.
546:     Let G be a finite group of odd order and suppose that a1 , . . . , ak ∈ G are pairwise distinct
547: and b1 , . . . , bk ∈ G are pairwise distinct. Snevily’s Conjecture states that there is a permuta-
548: tion π of the indices 1, 2, . . . , k for which a1 bπ(1) , a2 bπ(2) , . . . , ak bπ(k) are pairwise distinct. The
549: conjecture has been proved for cyclic groups of prime order by Alon [3], for cyclic groups by
550: Dasgupta, Károlyi, Serra, and Szegedy [8] and recently for all commutative groups by Arsovski
551: [5]. Note that Alon’s proof, which is based on the Combinatorial Nullstellensatz, allows one of
552: the sequences a1 , . . . , ak and b1 , . . . , bk to contain repeated elements.
553:      Our approach was the following. Let N be a normal subgroup of G. We look for the
554: permutation to be applied to the factor group G/N first. Of course, in the factor group, some
555: of the cosets a1 N, . . . , an N and b1 N, . . . , bn N may coincide to each other; it is even possible that
556: a1 N, . . . , an N all coincide and b1 N, . . . , bn N all coincide. So we must allow (ai N)(bπ(i) N) =
557: (aj N)(bπ(j) N) in some cases.
558:      The idea is to allow (ai N)(bπ(i) N) = (aj N)(bπ(j) N) only in those cases, when ai N = aj N
559: and bπ(i) N = bπ(j) N holds. Suppose that we found shuch a permutation π. If some classes
560: (ai1 N)(bπ(i1 ) N), (ai2 N)(bπ(i2 ) N), . . . , (aiℓ N)(bπ(iℓ ) N) coincide, then the elements ai1 , . . . , aiℓ are
561: all in the same coset cN, and similarly bπ(i1 ) , . . . , bπ(iℓ ) are in the same coset Nd. Then we could
562: apply Snevily’s Conjecture inductively to the sequences c−1 ai1 , . . . , c−1 aiℓ and bπ(i1 ) d−1 , . . . , bπ(iℓ ) d−1
563: which all lie in N. By the induction hypothesis, the values π(j1 ), . . . , π(jℓ ) can be permuted
564: 
565: 10                          Géza Kós, Tamás Mészáros, and Lajos Rónyai
566: 
567: 
568: in such a way, that c−1 ai1 bπ(i1 ) d−1 , . . . , c−1 aiℓ bπ(iℓ ) d−1 are pairwise distinct. Hence the elements
569: ai1 bπ(i1 ) , . . . , aiℓ bπ(iℓ ) are all in the coset (cd)N = cNd, and they will be pairwise distinct.
570:      If we choose N to be a maximal normal subgroup of G, then the order |G/N| = p will be a
571: prime (G is solvable, since |G| is odd). In this case we can use the additive group of the prime
572: field Fp and re-formulate the existence of the desired permutation.
573: 
574: Conjecture 11. Suppose that k ≤ p and a1 , . . . , ak ∈ Fp and b1 , . . . , bk ∈ Fp are arbitrary
575: elements. Then there is a permutation π of the indices 1, 2, . . . , k with the following property:
576: ai = aj and bπ(i) = bπ(j) holds whenever ai + bπ(i) = aj + bπ(j) .
577: 
578:    This conjecture would imply Snevily’s conjecture for all cases when k is not greater than
579: the smallest prime divisor of |G|.
580:    To use the polynomial method to prove Conjecture 11 and similar statements, it appears
581: that one is required to handle multiple values in the sequences a1 , . . . , ak and b1 , . . . , bk .
582: 
583:    The requirements in Theorems 2 and 4 about the invertibility in R of the differences s − s∗
584: can be somewhat relaxed. The theorems still hold if we assume, instead of invertibility, that
585: the multiplicative semigroup D ⊂ R generated by the differences s − s∗ and f(t1 ,...,tn ) does not
586: contain 0. In this case the map from R to its ring of quotients D −1 R lifts our proofs from D −1 R
587: to R (the reader is referred to [6] for basic facts on rings of quotients). Actually, it suffices to
588: assume that one specific product from D is not zero.
589:

535: 5     Concluding remarks
536: Our interest in developing a version of the nonvanishing theorem for multisets has grown out
537: of an attempt to prove Snevily’s conjecture [16] in a particular case.
538:     Let G be a ﬁnite group of odd order and suppose that a1 , . . . , ak ∈ G are pairwise distinct
539: and b1 , . . . , bk ∈ G are pairwise distinct. Snevily’s Conjecture states that there is a permuta-
540: tion π of the indices 1, 2, . . . , k for which a1 bπ(1) , a2 bπ(2) , . . . , ak bπ(k) are pairwise distinct. The
541: conjecture has been proved for cyclic groups of prime order by Alon [3], for cyclic groups by
542: Dasgupta, Károlyi, Serra, and Szegedy [8] and recently for all commutative groups by Arsovski
543: [5]. Note that Alon’s proof, which is based on the Combinatorial Nullstellensatz, allows one of
544: the sequences a1 , . . . , ak and b1 , . . . , bk to contain repeated elements when k < |G|.
545:      Our approach was the following. Let N be a normal subgroup of G. We look for the
546: permutation to be applied to the factor group G/N ﬁrst. Of course, in the factor group, some
547: of the cosets a1 N, . . . , an N and b1 N, . . . , bn N may coincide to each other; it is even possible that
548: a1 N, . . . , an N all coincide and b1 N, . . . , bn N all coincide. So we must allow (ai N)(bπ(i) N) =
549: (aj N)(bπ(j) N) in some cases.
550:      The idea is to allow (ai N)(bπ(i) N) = (aj N)(bπ(j) N) only in those cases, when ai N = aj N
551: and bπ(i) N = bπ(j) N holds. Suppose that we found such a permutation π. If some classes
552: (ai1 N)(bπ(i1 ) N), (ai2 N)(bπ(i2 ) N), . . . , (aiℓ N)(bπ(iℓ ) N) coincide, then the elements ai1 , . . . , aiℓ are
553: all in the same coset cN, and similarly bπ(i1 ) , . . . , bπ(iℓ ) are in the same coset Nd. Then we could
554: apply Snevily’s Conjecture inductively to the sequences c−1 ai1 , . . . , c−1 aiℓ and bπ(i1 ) d−1 , . . . , bπ(iℓ ) d−1
555: which all lie in N. By the induction hypothesis, the values π(j1 ), . . . , π(jℓ ) can be permuted
556: in such a way, that c−1 ai1 bπ(i1 ) d−1 , . . . , c−1 aiℓ bπ(iℓ ) d−1 are pairwise distinct. Hence the elements
557: ai1 bπ(i1 ) , . . . , aiℓ bπ(iℓ ) are all in the coset (cd)N = cNd, and they will be pairwise distinct.
558:      If we choose N to be a maximal normal subgroup of G, then the order |G/N| = p will be a
559: prime (G is solvable, since |G| is odd). In this case we can use the additive group of the prime
560: ﬁeld Fp and re-formulate the existence of the desired permutation.

566: elements. Then there is a permutation π of the indices 1, 2, . . . , k with the following property:
567: ai = aj and bπ(i) = bπ(j) holds whenever ai + bπ(i) = aj + bπ(j) .

569:    This conjecture would imply Snevily’s conjecture for all cases when k is not greater than

572: that one is required to handle multiple values in the sequences a1 , . . . , ak and b1 , . . . , bk .
573: 
574:    The requirements in Theorems 2 and 4 about the invertibility in R of the diﬀerences s − s∗
575: can be somewhat relaxed. The theorems still hold if we assume, instead of invertibility, that
576: the multiplicative monoid D ⊂ R generated by the diﬀerences s − s∗ and f(t1 ,...,tn ) does not
577: contain 0. In this case the map from R to its ring of fractions D −1 R lifts our proofs from D −1 R
578: to R (the reader is referred to [6] for basic facts on rings of fractions). Actually, it suﬃces to
579: assume that a certain speciﬁc product from D is not zero.

14:                                                     We provide an upper bound to the number of graph homomorphisms from G to H, where H
15:                                                 is a fixed graph with certain properties, and G varies over all N -vertex, d-regular graphs. This
16:                                                 result generalizes a recently resolved conjecture of Alon and Kahn on the number of independent
17:                                                 sets. We build on the work of Galvin and Tetali, who studied the number of graph homomor-
18:                                                 phisms from G to H when H is bipartite. We also apply our techniques to graph colorings and
19:                                                 stable set polytopes.
20: 
21: 
22:                                           1     Introduction
23:                                           1.1   From independent sets to graph homomorphisms
24:                                           Let G = (V, E) be a (simple, finite, undirected) graph. An independent set (or a stable set) is a
25:                                           subset of the vertices with no two adjacent. Let i(G) denote the number of independent sets of
26:                                           G. The following question is motivated by applications in combinatorial group theory [1, 12] and
27:                                           statistical mechanics [6].
29:                                           Question. In the family of N -vertex, d-regular graphs G, what is the maximum value of i(G)?
30: 
31:                                               Alon [1] first conjectured in 1991 that, when N is divisible by 2d, the maximum should be
32:                                           achieved when G is a disjoint union of complete bipartite graphs Kd,d . In 2001, Kahn [6] proved
33:                                           Alon’s conjecture in the case when G is a bipartite graph. Zhao [13] recently proved the conjecture
34:                                           in general. Theorem 1.1 contains a precise statement of the result. See [3] or [13] for a history of
35:                                           the problem.
36: 
37:                                           Theorem 1.1 (Zhao [13]). For any N -vertex, d-regular graph G,
38:                                                                                                          N/(2d)
39:                                                                          i(G) ≤ i (Kd,d )N/(2d) = 2d+1 − 1        .
40: 
41:                                           Note that there is equality when G is a disjoint union of Kd,d ’s.
42: 
43:                                               This result gives a tight upper bound to the quantity i(G)1/|V (G)| ranged over all d-regular
44:                                           graphs G. This quantity can be viewed as the number of independent sets normalized by the size
84:                                  hom(G, H) ≤ hom(Kd,d , H)N/(2d) .                                (1)
85: 
86:     Note that in contrast to Theorem 1.1, Theorem 1.2 requires G to be bipartite. It was conjectured
87: in [5] that the bipartite condition in Theorem 1.2 can be dropped. Unfortunately, this is false for
88: G = K3 and H a graph of two disconnected loops (see Example 2.3). We would like to know which
89: graphs H satisfy (1) for all G, as it would allow us to address the following question and thereby
90: to generalize Theorem 1.1 to other instances of graph homomorphisms.
91: 
92: Question. Let H be a fixed graph (allowing loops). In the family of N -vertex, d-regular graphs
93: G, what is the maximum value of hom(G, H)?
94: 
95: 1.2   Motivation of technique.
96: The proof of Theorem 1.1 consists of two main steps. The first step, given by Kahn [6], used entropy
97: methods to prove the theorem when G is bipartite. The second step, given by Zhao [13], reduces
240: Here we are interested in maximizing the number of q-colorings in the family of N -vertex, d regular
241: graphs.
242: Conjecture 2.7. For q ≥ 3, the complete graph Kq is GT. Equivalently, for any N -vertex, d-
243: regular graph G, the chromatic polynomial satisfies
244:                                       P (G, q) ≤ P (Kd,d , q)N/(2d) ,                              (3)
245: Note that we have equality when G is sa disjoint union of Kd,d ’s.
246:    From Theorem 1.2, we know that Conjecture 2.7 is true when G is bipartite. Although we
247: do not know how to prove the conjecture, we can show the following asymptotic result using our
248: bipartite swapping trick.
249: Theorem 2.8. For every N -vertex, d-regular graph G,
250:                                       P (G, q) ≤ P (Kd,d , q)N/(2d)
251: for all sufficiently large q (depending on N ). Note that equality occurs when G is a disjoint union
252: of Kd,d ’s.

713: Conjecture 5.1. Kq is strongly GT.
714: 
715:     Note that Conjecture 5.1 implies Conjecture 2.7. Since Kq is not a bipartite swapping target,
716: we cannot directly apply the bipartite swapping trick. However, it turns out that we can still use
717: the bipartite swapping trick to prove an asymptotic version of Conjecture 5.1. Here is the main
718: result of this section.

720: Proposition 5.2. Let G be a graph with N vertices. Then P (G ⊔ G, q) ≤ P (G × K2 , q) for
721: q ≥ (2N )2N +2 .
722: 
723:     Before we prove Proposition 5.2, let us deduce Theorem 2.8 from the Proposition.
724: 
725: Proof of Theorem 2.8. From Proposition 5.2, we have P (G, q)2 = P (G ⊔ G, q) ≤ P (G × K2 , q) for
726: sufficiently large q. Theorem 1.2 implies that P (G×K2 , q) = hom(G×K2 , Kq ) ≤ hom(Kd,d , Kq )N/d =
727: P (Kd,d , Kq )N/d for all q. Theorem 2.8 then follows from combining the two inequalities.

88: Acknowledgments. The author is indebted to Peter Sarnak for many years of
89: fruitful collaboration and friendship. Much of what is presented here was inspired
90: by him and in particular in Chapter 4 we have made extensive use of material
107: much more difficult. Various deep mathematical theories have been used to give
108: explicit constructions, e.g. Kazhdan property (T ) from representation theory of
109: semi-simple Lie groups and their discrete subgroups, the Ramanujan Conjecture
110: (proved by Deligne) from the theory of automorphic forms and more. All these
111: led to fascinating connections between pure mathematics and computer science
112: and between pure mathematicians and computer scientists. For the first three
113: decades – till approximately ten years ago – essentially all these connections
114: went in one direction: methods of pure mathematics have been used to solve
115: some problems arising from computer science (these are summarized in [L1] for
116: example).
117:     Something different is emerging in the last decade: Computer science pays its
118: debt to pure mathematics! The notion of expander graphs is starting to play a
119: significant role in more and more areas of pure mathematics.
120:     The goal of these notes is to describe expander graphs and their applications
554: prove their existence by random considerations. Various deep mathematical tools
555: have been used to give explicit constructions (Kazhdan property T , Ramanujan
556: conjecture, etc.). We review these briefly here (again, more details are in [L1] and
557: the references therein). Most of the current chapter is devoted to a description
558: of the two important developments of the last decade:
559: 
560:   1. All non-abelian finite simple groups are expanders in a uniform way, and
561: 
562:   2. Many linear groups have property (τ ) with respect to congruence subgroups.
563: 
564:    The second result has important applications to number theory, group theory
565: and geometry, to be described in later chapters.
566: 
567: 
572: here) is how to describe a good model of random (n, k)-graphs. Anyway this has
573: been known for many years (see [L1] and §1.1 above). More recently a much
574: deeper result (Alon’s conjecture) has been proved by Friedman [Fr].
575: 
576: Theorem 2.1. For every ε > 0 and k ≥ 3,
577:                                    √         
578:                      P rob λ(X) ≤ 2 k − 1 + ε = 1 − ok (1)
579: 
580: when X is a random (n, k)-graph, i.e., almost every such X is almost Ramanujan.
581: 
582:                                          14
583: 
584:      It is interesting to repeat here the open question mentioned in §1.2, whether
585: for every k ≥ 3 there exist infinitely many Ramanujan graphs. While Theorem 2.1
586: hints toward a positive answer, it by no means implies that. Moreover, one may
587: be tempted to conjecture, following Theorem 2.1, that almost every (n, k)-graph
588: is Ramanujan. But quite a lot of computational data suggests to the contrary,
589: namely that the probability of a k-regular graph on n vertices to be Ramanujan
590: tends, when n → ∞, to some constant strictly between 0 and 1. We do not know
591: any result or even a conjecture that predicts what is this interesting number.
592:      Of course, for any applications one wants explicit constructions. A lot of work
593: has been dedicated to this goal and various deep methods have been used. In a
594: breakthrough paper Reingold, Vadhan and Wigderson [RVW] showed that there
595: is an elementary combinatorial way to build expanders via the Zig-Zag product of
596: graphs, which they introduced. We describe this subject here very briefly sending
597: the reader to [RVW] for details (or to [HLW] for a very clear exposition).
598:      The Zig-Zag product is a method that given two graphs X and Y , where X
599: is an (n, m) graph (i.e., m-regular graph on n vertices) and Y an (m, d)-graph,
600: produces X ◦ Y , an (mn, d2 )-graphs. (This is not a commutative operation).
601: In [RVW], it is shown that one can bound the “spectral gap” of X ◦ Y by the
602: spectral gaps of X and Y . They then start for a small fixed integer d, with a
649: the prototype of finite simple groups of Lie type. Many more infinite families of
650: finite simple groups can be deduced, by a similar method, to be expanders. But a
651: more challenging conjecture was put forward in 1989 by Babai-Kantor-Lubotzky
652: [BKL]:
653: 
654: Conjecture 2.5. There exist k ∈ N and ε > 0 such that every non-abelian
655: finite simple group G has a symmetric set of generators Σ of size ≤ k such that
656: Cay(G; Σ) is an ε-expander.
657: 
658:     For a number of years this conjecture has been open and even some suspi-
659: cion arose (including by some of its proposers) that perhaps it is not true and
660: expansion is a property restricted to “bounded rank”. But it turned out that this
661: conjecture is true and it is now fully proved and its proof required an ensemble of
662: very different methods. The big breakthrough came with two works of Kassabov
663: [K1] and [K2] ([K1] was very much influenced by [KN] which in turn was modeled
664: on [Sh3]). Rather than being loyal to the historical development, let me start
665: with an even more recent result (which was influenced by [KN]). To state the
666: result we first need a definition. Let R be a ring with an identity. For n ≥ 3,
667: define En (R) to be the multiplicative subgroup of the ring of n × n matrices
668: Mn (R) generated by Eij (r) for all 1 ≤ i 6= j ≤ n and r ∈ R, where Eij (r) is the
669: n × n matrix with 1’s on the diagonal, r at the (i, j)-entry and zero otherwise.
670: For many commutative rings, En (R), at least for n ≥ 3, is nothing more than
671: SLn (R). The groups En (R) play an important role in algebraic K-theory.
672:     If R is a finitely generated ring then En (R) is a finitely generated group (for
707: The B is a bounded product of A.
708:     One can check in the proof that if one allows to use only SLn with n ≥ 3, the
709: result remains true for those groups in B of rank ≥ 14. Thus Conjecture 2.5 is
710: valid for these groups. To handle all finite simple groups of Lie type one should
711: handle SL2 (Fq ). This will be done in 2.4 by a completely different method. With
712: the above results this will finish all groups of Lie type except the Suzuki. They
713: will be handled in 2.6 again by a completely different method. But now we will
714: handle first the case of Symmetric and Alternating groups which is of special
715: interest.

988: ther. But shortly afterwards Bourgain and Gamburd [BG1] made a second major
989: breakthrough establishing the desired expansion by introducing their fundamen-
990: tal flattening lemma technique and coupling it with more standard techniques

1104: 2.7     Random generators and worst case generators
1105: In Sections 2.2, 2.3 and 2.4 we described how all the non-abelian finite simple
1106: groups, except for the Suzuki groups, can be made into a family of expanders
1107: uniformly. We showed that there exists k ∈ N and 0 < ε ∈ R such that for
1108: every such G there exists an explicitly given symmetric subset Σ of G of size at
1109: most k such that Cay(G; Σ) is an ε-expander. We did not bother to write the
1110: sets Σ explicitly but the method was explicit and if one wants, such a Σ can be
1111: presented. Now, for the Suzuki groups such Σ exists but in a non-explicit way.
1116: that for every Suzuki group G = Sz(22`+1 ), for almost every pair of elements
1117: (x, y) ∈ G × G, the Cayley graph Cay(G; {x±1 , y ±1 }) is an ε-expander.
1118:    So altogether Conjecture 2.5 is now a Theorem! This result handles the
1119: “best-case scenario”, i.e., there exists a set of generators Σ of G with the desired
1120: property. What about random set?
1121:    Recall the well known result:
1122: Theorem 2.27 ([D], [KL], [LiSh]). Two random elements of a finite simple group
1123: G generate G with probability going to 1 when |G| is going to infinity.
1124:     Another way to state the last result is that a random pair of elements gives
1125: rise to a connected Cayley graph. Is this graph expander? In a more precise
1126: formation.
1127: Open Problem 2.28. Is there an 0 < ε ∈ R, such that P rob(Cay(G; {x±1 , y ±1 })
1128: is ε-expander) is going to one when G runs over the non-abelian finite simple
1129: groups with |G| → ∞ and x and y are chosen randomly and uniformly from G?
1132: Lie rank. This of course generalizes Theorem 2.26 as well as a similar result which
1133: was known before for SL2 ([BG1] and [Di] using [GHSSV]). It can replace [L6]
1134: as a proof for the bounded case of Conjecture 2.5. (The proof in [L6] used deep
1135: results from automorphic forms like Selberg theorem and Drinfeld solution to the
1136: characteristic p Ramanujan conjecture - but gave explicit generators).
1137:     One can ask for even more: Is it possible that there exists ε > 0 such that
1138: Cay(G; {x±1 , y ±1 }) is ε-expander for every choice of generating set {x, y} for
1139: G and any non-abelian finite simple group (“worse case scenario”; compare to
1140: Babai conjecture 2.20). In the general case the answer is certainly - no! The
1141: family Alt(n) has generators which do not give rise to a family of expanders.
1142: (For Sym(n) one can take {τ = (1, 2), σ = (1, 2, . . . , n)}: Cay(Sym(n); {τ, σ ±1 })
1143: are not expanders - see [L1, Example 4.3.3(c)] - and from this one can deduce a
1144: similar result for Alt(n)). It seems likely that for a family of finite simple groups
1145: of unbounded rank, one can always find “worse case generators” which are not
1146: expanders. But one may suggest:
1147: Conjecture 2.29. For every r ∈ N there exists ε = ε(r) such that Cay(G; {x±1 , y ±1 })
1148: is an ε-expander for every finite simple group G of Lie type and rank at most r
1149: and for every generating set {x, y} of G.
1150:    One may want to compare Conjecture 2.29 with Corollary 2.23 which gives a
1151: weaker statement. An intermediate step would be to prove that
1152:                      diam Cay(G; {x±1 , y ±1 }) = Or (log |G|)
1153: 
1154:                                          26
1155: 
1156: where the implied constant depends only on r.
1157:    As of now the only result concerning Conjecture 2.29 is:
1158: 
1159: Theorem 2.30 (Breuillard-Gamburd [BGa]). There exists 0 < ε ∈ R and an
1160: infinite set of primes P such that for every p ∈ P and every generating set
1161: {x, y} of SL2 (Fp ), Cay(SL2 (Fp ); {x±1 , y ±1 }) is an ε-expander.
1162: 
1163:     Their interesting method is not explicit. They prove the existence of such a
1164: set P by a non effective method.

1439:     Another well known problem about primes is:
1440: 
1441: Conjecture 4.2 (Twin Prime Conjecture). There are infinitely many primes p,
1442: for which p + 2 is also a prime.

1444:    Another way to state the conjecture is: there are infinitely many x ∈ Z with
1445: ν(x(x + 2)) ≤ 2.

1447:                                        34
1448: 
1449:     One also expects that the Twin Prime Conjecture is true along arithmetic
1450: progressions satisfying the “local condition” above.
1451:     A far-reaching generalization is the next conjecture of Schinzel [SS], which
1452: needs some notations: Let Λ be an infinite subgroup of Z, i.e., Λ = qZ for some
1453: q 6= 0, and b ∈ Z. Let O be the orbit of b under the action of Λ on Z, i.e.,
1454: O = b + qZ. Let f (x) ∈ Q[x] be a polynomial which is integral on O. We say
1455: that the pair (O, f ) is primitive if for every 2 ≤ k ∈ Z there exists x ∈ O such
1456: that (f (x), k) = 1.
1457: 
1458: Conjecture 4.3 (Schinzel). If f (x) ∈ Q[x] is a product of t irreducible factors
1459: in Q[x], O = b + qZ as above, f is integral on O and (O, f ) is primitive, then
1460: there are infinitely many x ∈ O with ν(x) ≤ t.
1461: 
1462:      Taking f (x) = x, one gets Dirichlet Theorem and for f (x) = x(x+2) the Twin
1463: Prime Conjecture in its generalized form (also along arithmetic progressions).
1464:      There are various high-dimensional conjectures generalizing Dirichlet theo-
1465: rem. Let us set some more notations:
1466:      Let Λ be a non-trivial subgroup of Zn , b ∈ Zn and f (x1 , . . . , xn ) ∈ Q[x1 , . . . , xn ]
1467: which is integral on O = b + Λ. For r ∈ N, we denote by O(f, r) the set of x ∈ O
1468: for which ν(f (x)) ≤ r. We say that (O, f ) saturates if for some r < ∞, O(f, r)
1469: is Zariski dense in (the Zariski closure of) O. The smallest such r, if it exists at
1470: all, will be denoted r0 (O, f ).
1471: 
1472: Conjecture 4.4 (Hardy-Littlewood [HL]). Let Λ be a subgroup of Zn . Assume
1473: that for each j, the j-th coordinate function xj is nonconstant when restricted
1474: to Λ. Let b ∈ Zn , O = b + Λ and f (x) = x1 · x2 · . . . · xn , and assume (O, f ) is
1475: primitive. Then r0 (O, f ) = n, i.e., the set of x ∈ O whose all coordinates are
1476: simultaneously primes is Zariski dense in b + CΛ and in particular, it is infinite.
1477: 
1478:     A recent breakthrough of Green, Tao and Ziegler ([GTZ], [GT2]) has proved
1479: this conjecture for the case when rank(Λ) ≥ 2. The most difficult case is when
1480: rank(Λ) = 1. For example, by looking at b = (1, 3) ∈ Z2 and Λ = Z(1, 1), we see
1481: that the Twin Prime Conjecture is a special case.
1482:     Another special case is the following famous result proved not long ago by
1483: Green and Tao [GT1]:
1484: 
1485: Theorem 4.5 (Arithmetic progressions of primes). For every 3 ≤ k ∈ N, the set
1486: of primes contain an arithmetic progression of length k.
1487: 
1488:     To see that Theorem 4.5 is a special case of Conjecture 4.4, look at Zk and
1489: let Λ be the 2-dimensional subgroup Λ = Z(1, 1, 1, . . . , 1) + Z(0, 1, 2, 3, . . . , k − 1).
1490: Then the orbit of (1, 1, . . . , 1) is Λ which is the set {(m, m + n, m + 2n, . . . , m +
1491: (k − 1)n|m, n ∈ Z}. Conjecture 4.4 implies that there are infinitely many vectors
1492: of this kind whose entries are all primes.
1493: 
1494:                                                35
1495: 
1496:    The formulation of Hardy-Littlewood Conjecture 4.4, naturally suggests to
1497: study the existence of primes vectors (i.e., vectors whose all coordinates are
1498: primes) in the orbit Λ.b when this time Λ is a subgroup of GLn (Z). Somewhat
1499: surprisingly this has not been studied till recent years. It seems that counter
1500: examples of the following kind led to think that no real theory can be developed:
1501: 
1502: Example 4.6. Let Λ be the cyclic subgroup of SL2 (Z) generated by ( 78 67 ) and
1503: b = (1, 1)t . The orbit Λ.b is contained in {(x, y) ∈ Z2 |4x2 − 3y 2 = 1} from which
1504: one easily sees that no such y is a prime, in spite of the fact that for this problem
1505: there are no “local obstructions”.
1506: 
1507:    Another example of a similar flavor is:
1512: the orbit Λ.b. The orbit lies on the hyperbola {(x, y) ∈ Z2 |x2 − 3xy + y 2 = 1} and
1513: for n ∈ N we get the pairs (f2n−2 , f2n ), where fn is the n-th Fibonacci number
1514: (one can define them for n < 0 as well). While it is conjectured that infinitely
1515: many of the fn ’s are primes, f2n are not. In fact, f2n = fn ln when ln is the n-th
1516: Lucas number. Moreover, it is even expected that f2n has an unbounded number
1517: of prime factors, when n → ∞. (See [BLMS]).
1518: 
1519:     The exciting fact, which came out only in recent years, is that these examples
1520: are the exceptional, not the typical. The Zariski closure in these cases is a torus.
1521: We will see below how conjectures and results (!) like the Hardy-Littlewood
1522: conjecture have non-abelian analogues when a torus is not involved. The key new
1523: ingredient for all this are the expanders combined with the classical combinatorial
1524: sieve of Brun. This will be our topic in the next section.
1525: 
1526: 
1527: 4.2     Brun sieve and expanders
1528: For 0 < x ∈ QR, denote by P(x) the set of primes smaller or equal x, π(x) = |P(x)|
1529: and P (x) = p∈P(x) p. Evaluating π(x) is one of the most important problems in
1530: mathematics, if not the most important. Well, the prime number theorem says
1531:               x
1532: that π(x) ∼ logx  and the Riemann hypothesis gives a sharp bound for the error
1533: term in this asymptotic result. In fact, one has an exact formula for π(x) which
1561: reader is referred to [FI] and [IK], for example. Let us say a few words about
1562: the combinatorial sieve developed by Brun. His main motivation was to han-
1563: dle the Twin Prime Conjecture 4.2. In a different language it says that if
1564: f (x) = x(x + 2) then for infinitely many n’s in N, f (n) is a product of only
1565: two primes, i.e. ν(f (n)) ≤ 2. Let f (x) be any integral polynomial f (x) ∈ Z[x]
1566: e.g., f (x) = x(x + 2). Let x be a large real number, and z < x. Denote:
1567:                                              X
1568:                               S(f, z) :=           1
1569:                                                       n≤x
1570:                                                 (f (n),P (z))=1
1571: 
1572: so S(f, z) counts those n less than x, such that f (n) is not divisible by any prime
1573: less than z. Of course, we want z to be as large as possible.
1574:     Recall the Mobius function
1622: 
1623: divisor for f (n) less than nδ . For such n’s, ν(f (n)) ≤ degδ f . So, while he felt
1624: short from proving the Twin Prime Conjecture he was able to show that there
1625: are infinitely many n’s with ν(f (n)) ≤ 18. His method has been refined; the
1626: current record is due to Chen [Ch] who replaced 18 by 3. Namely, there are
1627: infinitely many pairs (n, n + 2) such that one of them is a prime and the other is
1628: a product of at most two primes.
1629:     These “combinatorial sieve methods” have also been applied to the higher
1630: dimensional cases described in §4.1. For examples, one gets a partial result toward
1631: Hardy-Littlewood Conjecture: in the notation of Conjecture 4.4, on can prove
1632: that there exists r ∈ N such that r0 (O, f ) ≤ r. In particular, the orbit b +
1633: Λ contains infinitely many vectors all whose entries are product of a bounded
1634: number of primes. Moreover, it has even been proved that this r depends only
1635: on n and not on b or Λ (assuming, of course, no local obstructions, i.e. (O, f ) is
1636: primitive).
1637:     All this is a quite deep (and quite technical) theory. The relevant for our story
1638: came from the insight of Sarnak who noticed that the machinery of the Brun sieve
1639: can be carried out also for a general non-commutative subgroups Λ ≤ GLn (Z)
1640: acting of Zn , provided Λ has property (τ ) w.r.t congruence subgroups. The orbit
1641: in this case of b ∈ Zn is Λ.b = {γ.b|γ ∈ Γ}, and one can start the same kind
1642: of computation we illustrated above for the Twin Prime problem. This time,
1682: called “affine sieve” as it also covers “affine transformations” of Zn and not only
1683: linear. The affine case can be easily reduced to a linear case of higher dimen-
1684: sions. Some of the classical problems (e.g. the Hardy-Littlewood Conjecture) are
1685: naturally expressed as affine problems rather than linear.
1686:     The case which is not covered by the last general theorem is of a torus (or
1687: when one has central torus in G). Some of the difficult problems in number
1688: theory can be presented in this language: e.g. the Mersenne Conjecture: there
1689: are infinitely many primes p with 2p − 1 also a prime, is such a problem (See
1690: [BGS2, §2.1]). But the set of primes is “too thin” to sieve over it. So the new
1691: method shed no new light on this conjecture. It is not even known if there are
1692: infinitely many almost primes of the form 2n − 1.
1693:     Still there are few concrete problems where the new method gives some fas-
1694: cinating results. Some of them will be described in the next section.
1695: 
1696:                                          39
1697: 
1698: 4.3     Some applications to classical problems
1699: Theorem 4.9 above gives some results which are completely out of reach by other
1700: methods. E.g., if Λ ≤ GLn (Z) is a group as in the theorem, then the group
1701: itself contained infinitely many matrices which are almost primes, i.e., all their
1702: entries are products of a bounded number of primes. An example satisfying this
1972: of elements of MCG (1) = SL2 (Z) into hyperbolic, parabolic and elliptic. We will
1973: not give the exact definitions sending the reader to [Ri], [Ko1] and the references
1974: therein for details. He conjectured that “generic” elements of M are pseudo-
1975: Anosov. In one form (which is weaker and stronger than the following theorem)
1976: this was proved by Maher ([Ma1], [Ma2]). Rivin [Ri] (see also Kowalski [Ko1])
1977: proved, by using the sieve method:
1978: 
1979: Theorem 5.4. The set of pseudo-Anosov elements of M = MCG (g) is exponen-
1980: tially generic.
1981: 
1982:     The proof uses the fact that M = MCG (g) is mapped onto the arithmetic
1983: group Γ = Sp (2g, Z). Now, a criterion due to Casson and Bleiler gives a sufficient
1984: condition for an element γ of M to be pseudo-Anosov in terms of some conditions
1985: on its image γ 0 ∈ Γ. Rivin showed that the set of those γ 0 ∈ Γ which do not satisfy
2103: what unexpectedly, in geometry. Most of these applications are for hyperbolic
2104: manifolds. The background is given in §6.1. Then in §6.2 we will give the first ap-
2105: plication: a proof given in [L3] using expanders and property (τ ) of a conjecture of
2106: Thurston and Waldhausen on positive virtual Betti number for arithmetic hyper-
2107: bolic manifolds. Then in §6.3 we describe the attack of Lackenby on the “virtual
2108: Haken conjecture” for hyperbolic 3-manifolds using expanders (or more precisely
2109: the Lubotzky-Sarnak conjecture asserting that 3-manifolds hyperbolic groups do
2110: not have (τ )). While, as of now, this attack has not led to a complete solution of
2111: the virtual Haken conjecture, it led to some partial results and opened exciting
2112: new directions. In particular, it shows connections between Heegaard genus of
2113: 3-manifolds and expanders. This will be elaborated further in §6.4. We will show
2114: there another application of expanders to hyperbolic 3-manifolds. Moreover, the
2115: notion of cost from dynamics will be related to 3-manifolds via expanders!
2116: 
2117: 
2118: 6.1     Hyperbolic manifolds
2119: Let M be an oriented n-dimensional hyperbolic manifold of finite volume. Such a
2120: manifold is obtained from the Lie group G = SO(n, 1)- the group of (n+1)×(n+1)
2121: real matrices preserving the quadratic form X12 +. . .+Xn2 −Xn+12   - in the following
2122: way: Let K = SO(n) sitting as a maximal compact subgroup of G and Γ a torsion-
2126: questions on such M can be translated to group theoretic questions about Γ which
2127: is actually isomorphic to the fundamental group of M as Hn is contractible.
2128:     One of these questions is the following conjecture usually attributed to Thurston
2129: (though it probably goes back to Waldhausen):
2130: 
2131:                                          49
2132: 
2133: Conjecture 6.1 (Thurston-Waldhausen Conjecture). The manifold M has a
2134: finite sheeted cover M0  M with positive β1 (M0 ) := dim H1 (M0 , R), i.e.non-
2135: trivial homology group. Or, equivalently, in group theoretic terms: every lattice
2136: Γ in SO(n, 1) has a finite index subgroup Γ0 with |Γ0 /[Γ0 , Γ0 ]| = ∞.
2137:     The equivalence follows from two well-known facts: every lattice is finitely
2138: generated and has a torsion free subgroup of finite index. The commutator quo-
2139: tient is infinite iff there is a surjective map Γ0  Z and this happens iff the first
2140: real homology of Γ0 \ G/K is non-trivial.
2141:     Let us mention right at the start another conjecture, due to Serre [Se] (which
2142: is now almost fully proved - see §6.2 below).
2143: Conjecture 6.2 (Serre Conjecture). If Γ is an arithmetic lattice of G = SO(n, 1),
2144: then Γ has a negative answer to the congruence subgroup property.
2145:     It is well known that the Thurston-Waldhausen conjecture implies Serre’s
2146: conjecture. This can be seen in one of the following ways: (i) if Γ has the
2147: congruence subgroup property then its profinite completion Γ̂ is the same as
2148: the congruence completion. The latter is a product of compact p-adic analytic
2149: semisimple groups and as such, a finite index subgroup of it should have finite
2150: abelianization. Thus the same applies to Γ̂ and Γ. (ii) It is known that the
2151: congruence subgroup property for Γ implies super-rigidity (cf. [Se]) but if Γ
2152: virtually maps onto Z it does not have superigidity.
2153:     An intermediate step between these two conjectures is:
2154: Conjecture 6.3 (Lubotzky-Sarnak Conjecture). If Γ is a lattice in SO(n, 1) then
2155: Γ does not have property (τ ).
2156:     Now, Thurston-Waldhausen conjecture ⇒ Lubotzky-Sarnak conjecture ⇒
2157: Serre conjecture. Indeed, if finite index subgroup of Γ is mapped onto Z then
2158: it clearly does not have (τ ) as Z does not have (τ ). Also, we mentioned in §2.5
2159: that an arithmetic lattice Γ always has (τ ) with respect to congruence subgroups.
2160: Thus, if Γ does not have (τ ), it must have also non-congruence subgroups.
2161:     This last observation was the key point in [L3] to be described in §6.2. But
2162: before going into these details, let us continue with another conjecture for the
2163: special case, n = 3, which is the most interesting case:
2164: Conjecture 6.4 (Virtual Haken Conjecture). A finite volume hyperbolic 3-
2165: manifold M is virtually Haken, i.e., has a finite sheeted cover which is Haken
2166: (also known as “sufficiently large”).
2167:     Recall that Haken means that it contains an incompressible surface, i.e., a
2168: properly embedded orientable surface S (other than S 2 ) with π1 (S) injecting
2169: into π1 (M ). It is known that M is Haken iff π1 (M ) is either mapped onto Z
2170: or π1 (M ) is a free product with amalgam in a non-trivial way i.e. iff π1 (M )
2171: 
2172:                                          50
2173: 
2174: splits, in the terminology of §5.5. From this it is clear that Thurston-Waldhausen
2175: conjecture for n = 3 implies the virtual Haken conjecture.
2176: 
2177: 
2178: 6.2    Thurston-Waldhausen conjecture for hyperbolic
2179:        arithmetic manifolds
2180: The first use of expanders in geometry came out in the proof of the following
2181: result in [L3]:
2182: 
2183: Theorem 6.5. Conjecture 6.1 is true for arithmetic lattices in SO(n, 1) for n 6=
2184: 3, 7. Namely, every finite volume n-dimensional arithmetic hyperbolic manifold
2185: has a finite sheeted cover with positive first Betti number if n 6= 3, 7.
2186: 
2187:      The result covers also “most” of the arithmetic lattices in SO(3, 1) and
2188: SO(7, 1). But these two cases are exceptional in having “more” arithmetic lat-
2189: tices than what one finds in SO(n, 1) for other n’s. The reasons are: SO(3, 1)
2190: is locally isomorphic to SL2 (C) and as such also has a complex structure, unlike
2191: all other n’s. On the other hand SO(7, 1) is a real form of SO(8). The later has
2192: Dynkin diagram of type D4 and as such also has a graph automorphism of order
2193: 3 (“the triality of D4 ”) unlike the other Dn ’s which have only automorphisms of
2194: order 2. The theory of “Galois cohomology” which enables one to classify the
2197: exist for other n’s. The method of proof of Theorem 6.5 does not apply to these
2198: extra families.
2199:      The connection between Conjecture 6.1 and expanders (or more precisely
2200: property (τ )) is best explained via the following:
2201: 
2202: Lemma 6.6 (The Sandwich Lemma). Assume G1 ≤ G2 ≤ G3 are three non-
2203: compact simple Lie groups and for each i = 1, 2, 3, Γi is an arithmetic lattice in
2204: Gi such that Γ1 ≤ Γ2 ≤ Γ3 , Γ2 = G2 ∩ Γ3 and Γ1 = G1 ∩ Γ3 (= G1 ∩ Γ2 ). Then
2205: 
2206:  (i) If Γ1 has the Selberg property (i.e. property (τ ) w.r.t. congruence subgroups
2207:      - see Definition 2.14) and Γ3 does not have (τ ), then Γ2 has negative answer
2208:      to the congruence subgroup problem (i.e., has non congruence subgroups).
2209: 
2210:  (ii) If Γ1 has the Selberg property and Γ3 has a congruence subgroup Λ with an
2230: an arithmetic lattices in SO(n, 1), n 6= 3, 7, it has a congruence subgroup which
2231: is mapped onto Z. In particular, it does not satisfy (τ ) (so Lubotzky-Sarnak
2232: Conjecture 6.3 is also valid) and does not have the congruence subgroup property
2233: (so Serre’s conjecture 6.2 is also true for these cases.)
2234:     All these three conjectures are still open for the lattices of SO(7, 1) coming
2235: from the triality effect of D4 . The story of n = 3 is more involved and more
2236: important. This is our topic in the next section. We just mention in passing
2237: that the Thurston-Waldhausen conjecture has been proved for the known non-
2238: arithmetic lattices in SO(n, 1) for n ≥ 4 (see [L4]). It is still widely open for
2239: others (if they exist at all ...).
2240: 
2241: 
2242: 6.3    Hyperbolic 3-manifolds
2243: A few years ago, Marc Lackenby initiated a program to prove the virtual Haken
2244: conjecture for hyperbolic 3-manifolds (Conjecture 6.4 above). In his program,
2245: expanders (or property τ ) play a central role, as well as the notion of Heegaard
2246: splitting. Let us recall the definition of the latter.
2247:     Let M be a connected, closed, orientable and irreducible (i.e. any 2-dimensional
2248: sphere in M bounds a 3-dimensional ball) 3-manifold. We will be mainly inter-
2249: ested in hyperbolic 3-manifolds, i.e., the case when M = Γ\H3 where Γ is a
2250: cocompact torsion free lattice in G = P SL2 (C).
2251:     A classical result asserts that every such M can be decomposed as a union of
2252: two handle bodies M = H1 ∪ H2 glued along their (isomorphic) boundaries, i.e.
2253: H1 ∩ H2 = ∂H1 = ∂H2 . This decomposition (Heegaard Splitting) is not unique.
2254: The minimal number g of handles in H1 (or H2 - they are isomorphic) in such a
2255: decomposition is called the Heegaard genus of M , denoted g(M ). Note that if H1
2283: damental group of M is bounded above by the Heegaard genus.
2284:    For general 3-manifolds this can be a strict inequality, but:
2285: Conjecture 6.9 (Heegaard genus versus rank conjecture). If M is a compact
2286: hyperbolic 3-manifold, then d(π1 (M )) = g(M ).
2287:     Now, if M0 is an r-sheeted cover of M , then the Heegaard splitting of M can
2288: be lifted to M0 and one can deduce that g(M0 ) ≤ rg(M ). To “renormalize” this,
2289: define:
2290: Definition 6.10. Let Γ = π1 (M ), L = {Ni } a family of finite index subgroups
2291: of Γ and {Mi } the corresponding finite sheeted covers. The infimal Heegaard
2292: gradient χhL (M ) of M w.r.t. L is defined as: χhL (M ) = inf 2g(M i )−2
2293:                                                              
2294:                                                                 [Γ:Ni ]  .
2295:                                                          i
2296: 
2301: of (all) finite index subgroups of π1 (M ). This happens for example if M fibres
2302: over a circle (or virtually fibres over a circle). There are many examples of such
2303: hyperbolic 3-manifolds. In fact, a conjecture attributed to Thurston suggests
2304: that every hyperbolic 3-manifold fibres over a circle after passing to a suitable
2305: finite sheeted cover. This is even stronger than Conjecture 6.1 above. In group
2306: 
2307:                                         53
2308: 
2309: theoretic terms it is equivalent to the assertion that Γ = π1 (M ) has a finite index
2310: subgroup Γ0 with an epimorphism π : Γ0  Z whose kernel Ker(π) is finitely
2311: generated. In this case Ker(π) must be a surface group of genus g and going
2312: along the cyclic covers between Γ0 and Ker(π) we get infinitely many covers with
2313: the same Heegaard genus. Hence χhL (M ) = 0.
2314:     Lackenby conjectured that this is the only reason for the Heegaard gradient
2315: to vanish, i.e.,
2316: 
2317: Conjecture 6.11 (Heegaard gradient conjecture). If M has a family of covers
2318: L with χhL (M ) = 0, then M virtually fibres over a circle.
2319: 
2320:    He then proved:
2321: 
2322: Theorem 6.12. Let M be a closed, orientable 3-manifold and L = {Ni } a family
2323: finite index normal subgroups, with corresponding covers {Mi }. Assume:
2324: a) χhL (M ) > 0
2325: b) Γ = π1 (M ) does not have property (τ ) w.r.t L.
2326:     Then M is virtually Haken.
2327: 
2328:    This last result implies:
2329: 
2330: Corollary 6.13. The Lubotzky-Sarnak Conjecture (Conjecture 6.3) and the Hee-
2331: gaard gradient Conjecture (Conjecture 6.11) imply the virtual Haken Conjecture
2332: (Conjecture 6.4.)
2333: 
2334:     Indeed let M be a 3-dimensional hyperbolic manifold and L the family of all its
2335: finite index normal subgroups. By the Lubotzky-Sarnak Conjecture Γ = π1 (M )
2336: does not have (τ )-so condition (b) of Theorem 6.12 is satisfied. If χhL (M ) > 0
2337: then M is virtually Haken by this Theorem, while if χhL (M ) = 0 then by the
2338: Heegaard gradient Conjecture, M virtually fibres over a circle and in particular
2339: virtually Haken.
2340:     This puts property (τ ) and expanders at the heart of the theory of 3-dimensional
2341: manifolds. As of now, the full virtual Haken conjecture has not been proven, but
2342: this approach, beside its intrinsic interest, led to some interesting unconditional
2343: results - cf. [La3], [La4], [LaLR1], [LaLR2].
2344: 
2345: 
2346: 6.4     Heegaard splitting, property (τ ) and cost
2347: In the previous section we saw the result of Lackenby, Theorem 6.7, which con-
2348: nects Heegaard genus and the Cheeger constant. The Cheeger constant is the ge-
2349: ometric way to express expanders (see Theorem 1.20). These connections enabled
2350: Long, Lubotzky and Reid [LLR] to deduce the following geometric application of
2351: the theory of expanders.
2352: 
2365: 
2366:     It should be stressed that in many examples of M ’s as in Theorem 6.14, (and
2367: if the Thurston-Waldhausen Conjecture 6.1 is correct, then in all such M ’s!) one
2368: can also find a chain of normal subgroups L0 in π1 (M ) with χhL0 (M ) = 0. This
2369: shows that the Heegaard gradient does depend on the choice of chains of normal
2370: covers (even chains with trivial intersections).
2371: 
2372:     This brings us to another fascinating connection: the notion of cost.
2373:     Let Γ be a countable group acting ergodically on X, a standard Borel space,
2374: by Borel automorphisms preserving a probability measure µ on X. Define the
2375: equivalence relation E on X by xEy iff x and y are on the same Γ-orbit. So E
2376: is a subset of X × X, which can be thought as defining a graph on X. For an
2377: arbitraryR Borel subset S of X × X we denote degS (x) = |{y ∈ X|(x, y) ∈ S}| and
2378: e(s) =      degS (x)dµ. (See [Gab2]).
2388:     This notion was introduced by Levitt [Le] and was used by Gaboriau [Gab1]
2389: to distinguish between equivalence relations of different group actions. Gaboriau
2390: conjectured:
2391: 
2392: Conjecture 6.16 (Fixed Price Conjecture). Given Γ, then Cost(Γ, X) is the
2393: same number for all ergodic, essentially free actions of Γ on a standard Borel
2394: space X. (Essentially free means that the set of x ∈ X with non-trivial stabilizer
2395: in Γ is of measure zero).
2396: 
2397:     Gaboriau proved this conjecture for various groups but it is still widely open
2398: for general Γ.
2399:     An interesting example in which the cost was computed explicitly is:
2400: 
2401:                                        55
2402: 
2403: Theorem 6.17 (Abert-Nikolov [AN]). Let Γ be a finitely generated group, L =
2404: {Ni }i∈N a chain of finite index normal subgroups N1 ⊇ N2 ≥ · · · with ∩Ni = {e}
2405:                                                                             i
2406: and Γ̄ = limΓ/Ni , the profinite completion of Γ w.r.t. L. The group Γ acts freely
2407:             ←
2408: on Γ̄ and
2412:                                                                         i
2413: 
2414:    Now, if the Fixed Price Conjecture is true it would follow that the rank
2415: gradient of Γ does not depend on the chain L in the last theorem. On the other
2416: hand we saw above that for Γ = π1 (M ), M a 3-dimensional hyperbolic compact
2417: manifold the Heegaard genus gradient does depend on the choice of L. This
2418: enabled Abert and Nikolov to deduce:
2419: 
2420: Theorem 6.18. At least one of the two conjectures: the Heegaard genus versus
2421: rank conjecture (Conjecture 6.9) and the Fixed price conjecture (Conjecture 6.16)
2422: is not true!
2423: 
2424:     It is quite interesting how these two seemingly unrelated conjectures contra-
2425: dict each other and for our story it is also interesting how this contradiction is
2426: via property (τ ).
2427:     Of course, it might be that both conjectures are false! One may even speculate
2428: that the fixed price conjecture is false in general but it is true for hyperbolic
2429: groups, just as it is true for free groups ([Gab1]). If this is the case, or even if it
2430: is true for the much smaller class of fundamental groups of compact hyperbolic
2431: 3-manifolds, then the Heegaard genus versus rank conjecture would be refuted.
2432:

2436:                                           56
2437: 
2438: Chapter 7
2439: 
2440: Miscellaneous
2441: 
2442: As mentioned in the introduction, expander graphs have a huge number of ap-
2447: found their way into the main chapters.
2448: 
2449: (I) The Baum-Connes conjecture: This is a famous deep conjecture. For
2450: a user-friendly introduction see [Va3]. Counterexamples to a generalized form of
2451: it were given in [Gro1] and [HLS]. The original conjecture is still open though
2452: it was proved for many classes of groups. The counterexamples were given by
2453: random groups constructed via expanders.
2454: 
2455: (II) Embedding metric spaces: There is great interest in embedding (finite)
2456: metric spaces into Hilbert spaces in a way that the metric is more or less preserved.
2457: In recent years this area has found many applications in computer science - see
2458: [HLW, Chap. 13]. Expander graphs play the role of graphs whose metric is the
2459: farthest away from euclidean.
2460: 
2461: (III) Dimension expanders: The notion of expander graphs have an ana-
2462: logue in vector spaces. For a fixed field F and 0 < ε, we say that T1 , . . . , Tk ∈

56: the efficiency of the new method we are able to apply it in order to give answers
57: to several conjectures which have been recently stated in combinatorics, number
58: theory, and the theory of formal languages.

63: 
64: 1 Introduction and background
65: 
66: The joint spectral characteristics of linear operators are now applied in many
67: areas, from functional analysis and dynamical systems to discrete mathematics
68: and number theory. We focus on two characteristics: the joint spectral radius and
414: coding theory and number theory. In the problem of asymptotic growth of the
415: number of overlap-free words (§8.1) we compute precise values of exponents of the
416: upper and lower growth. This proves two conjectures stated in 2008 [JPB2]. Then
417: in §8.2 we do the same for the problem of density of ones in the Pascal rhombus,
418: and disprove one previously know conjecture. In §8.3 and §8.4 we find precise
419: values of the lower and upper growths of the Euler partition functions for some
420: values of the parameters.
421:      Section 9 presents the results of numerical tests for JSR and LSR computation
422: for randomly generated matrices of dimensions from 5 to 100. In all the cases
423: the algorithms find the exact values of JSR and LSR, which suggests that our
424: approach generically has finite convergence.
425:      In the sequel we assume that the basis {ei }di=1 of the space Rd is fixed and do
426: not distinguish between operators and the corresponding matrices. An eigenvalue λ
427: is simple, if it is of multiplicity 1. The largest by modulo eigenvalue of an operator
428: B is called leading and denoted by λmax (if there are several such eigenvalues, then
429: 
2000: of bigger dimensions (up to d = 50) arising in various problems of combinatorics
2001: and number theory. Finding the exact values of JSR and LSR we prove, in par-
2002: ticular, several previously stated conjectures in combinatorics and number theory,
2003: and disprove one conjecture on Pascal’s rhombus. Further, in Section 9, we show
2004: the statistics how our algorithms work for randomly generated matrices of various
2005: dimensions. For all randomly generated families the algorithms found the exact
2006: values of JSR and of LSR (the latter is in the case of nonnegative families).

2012: 
2013: We consider here some simple examples showing the flow of the algorithms we
2014: have presented both for the joint spectral radius and for the lower spectral radius
2241: the literature (see the recent survey [Be]). In [C] and then in [JPB2] this problem
2242: was reduced to computing JSR and LSR of two special nonnegative 20 × 20-
2243: matrices. Those values were computed approximately, and two conjectures were
2244: stated about their exact values [JPB2]. Now we prove both those conjectures by
2245: applying Algorithms (P) and (L).

2250:      A binary word, i.e., a finite sequence of zeros and ones, is called overlap-free
2251: if it does not contain a subword of the form xaxax, where x ∈ {0, 1} and a is a
2252: word. In 1906 Thue proved that there are infinitely many such words. A natural
2253: problem, which was analyzed in many papers, is to estimate the total number un
2254: of overlap-free words of length n. In 1988 Brlek showed that un ≥ 3 n − 3, on
2255: the other hand Restivo and Salemi in 1985 proved the polynomial upper bound

9:                                                   A BSTRACT. A good edge-labeling of a graph [Araújo, Cohen, Giroire, Havet, Discrete
10:                                                   Appl. Math., forthcoming] is an assignment of numbers to the edges such that for no pair
11:                                                   of vertices, there exist two non-decreasing paths. In their paper Araújo et al. conjecture that,
12:                                                   if the average degree of G is less than 3 and G is minimal without an edge-labeling, then
13:                                                   G ∈ {C3 , K2,3 }. In this paper, we prove this conjecture under the additional hypothesis
14:                                                   that the girth of G is at least five.
15: 
16: 
17: 
18: 
19:                                                                                      1. I NTRODUCTION
20:                                             All graphs are finite and simple. We refer to Diestel [7] for most of our graph theory
21:                                          terminology.
22:                                             A good edge-labeling [10] of a graph G is a labeling of its edges φ : E(G) → R such
23:                                          that, for any ordered pair of vertices u and v, there is at most one nondecreasing path from
24:                                          u to v. We will mostly use the following characterization of a good edge-labeling, which
54: cuts. One of the central contributions of our paper is that we move beyond using matching-
55: cuts.
56:    Araujo et al. also pose a number of problems and conjectures. In particular, they have
57: the following conjecture, which is one of the two motivations behind our paper.
58: 
59: Conjecture 1.1 (Araujo et al. [2]). There is no critical graph with average degree less than
60: three, with the exception of C3 and K2,3 .
61: 
62:    Araujo et al. [2] prove a weaker version of this conjecture. They establish the existence
63: of a matching-cut, relying in part on a theorem by Farley & Proskurowski [8, 6] stating that
64: a graph with sufficiently few edges always has a matching cut. They also use a characteriza-
65: tion of extremal graphs with no matching-cut by Bonsma [5, 6]. From the proofs in Araujo
66: et al. [2], it appears that the depths of the arguments increases rapidly as the upper bound 3
67: is approached.
68:    In this paper, we show that there is no critical graph with average degree less than three
69: and girth at least five. Put differently, we prove Conjecture 1.1 in the case when the graph
70: has girth at least five.
71: 
72: Theorem 1.2. There is no critical graph with average degree less than three and girth at
73: least five.
74: 
75:     The second motivation behind our paper is the fact that no bad graph with girth larger
76: than four is known. (In particular, the bad graphs in Araujo et al.’s construction contain
77: many 4-cycles.) In fact, we conjecture that there exists a finite number g such that every
78: graph with girth at least g has a good edge-labeling. As mentioned above, Araujo et al. [2]
79: have shown that with the additional restriction that the graphs be planar, g := 6 does the
80: trick. In Theorem 5.2, roughly speaking, we prove that a critical graph with girth at least five
81: cannot contain a “windmill”. A windmill essentially consists of a number of shortest paths
82: meeting in an “axis”, with the paths originating from vertices of degree two and having
83: in their interior only vertices of degree three. Theorem 1.2 is a corollary of Theorem 5.2:
84: using an approach inspired by the discharging method from topological graph theory, we
85: argue that a hypothetical critical graph with girth at least five and average degree less than
86: three always contains a windmill.
87:     For our proof of Theorem 5.2, we define a class of graphs which we call “decent”, which
88: have the property that they cannot be contained in a critical graph. More importantly, we
927: good edge-labelings, or even decent edge-labelings. In this paper, we have used this ap-
928: proach together with a degree-bound. It seems probable that high girth benefits other open
929: problems about good edge-labeling. For example, Araújo et al. [2] conjecture that for every
930: 
931:                                          GOOD EDGE-LABELINGS                                             21
932: 
933: c < 4, the number of (pairwise non-isomorphic) critical graphs with average degree at most
934: c is finite. We propose the following weakening of their conjecture.
935: Conjecture 7.1. For every c < 4, the number of (pairwise non-isomorphic) critical graphs
936: with girth at least five and average degree at most c is finite.
937:    In a follow-up paper, we will use the techniques developed in this paper to prove Con-
938: jecture 7.1 for c = 3.
939:    A more basic question presents itself at this point. As mentioned in the introduction, no
940: examples of bad graphs with girth at least five are known. We pose the following question.
941: Question 7.2. Is there a constant g such that every graph with girth at least g has a good
942: edge-labeling?
943:   We are currently working on constructing bad graphs with girth five. Moreover, we do
944: not believe that g := 6 is sufficient for Question 7.2, and we pose as a third problem to
945: construct bad graphs of girth six.
946: 

FILE arxiv.org_pdf_1109.1125v2.txt
11:                                                   of vertices, there exist two non-decreasing paths. In this paper, we study edge-labeling on
12:                                                   graph graphs with girth at least five. In particular we verify, under this additional hypoth-

19:                                                                                     1. I NTRODUCTION
20:                                             All graphs are finite and simple. We refer to Diestel [7] for most of our graph theory
21:                                          terminology.
22:                                             A good edge-labeling [10] of a graph G is a labeling of its edges φ : E(G) → R such
23:                                          that, for any ordered pair of vertices u and v, there is at most one nondecreasing path from
24:                                          u to v. We will mostly use the following characterization of a good edge-labeling, which
54: cuts. One of the central contributions of our paper is that we move beyond using matching-
55: cuts.
56:    Araujo et al. also pose a number of problems and conjectures. In particular, they have
57: the following conjecture, which is one of the two motivations behind our paper.
58: 
59: Conjecture 1.1 (Araujo et al. [2]). There is no critical graph with average degree less than
60: three, with the exception of C3 and K2,3 .
61: 
62:    Araujo et al. [2] prove a weaker version of this conjecture. They establish the existence
63: of a matching-cut, relying in part on a theorem by Farley & Proskurowski [8, 6] stating that
64: a graph with sufficiently few edges always has a matching cut. They also use a characteriza-
65: tion of extremal graphs with no matching-cut by Bonsma [5, 6]. From the proofs in Araujo
66: et al. [2], it appears that the depths of the arguments increases rapidly as the upper bound 3
67: is approached.
68:    In this paper, we show that there is no critical graph with average degree less than three
69: and girth at least five. Put differently, we prove Conjecture 1.1 in the case when the graph
70: has girth at least five.
71: 
72: Theorem 1.2. There is no critical graph with average degree less than three and girth at
73: least five.
74: 
75:     The second motivation behind our paper is the fact that no bad graph with girth larger
76: than four is known. (In particular, the bad graphs in Araujo et al.’s construction contain
77: many 4-cycles.) In fact, we conjecture that there exists a finite number g such that every
78: graph with girth at least g has a good edge-labeling. As mentioned above, Araujo et al. [2]
79: have shown that with the additional restriction that the graphs be planar, g := 6 does the
80: trick. In Theorem 5.2, roughly speaking, we prove that a critical graph with girth at least five
81: cannot contain a “windmill”. A windmill essentially consists of a number of shortest paths
82: meeting in an “axis”, with the paths originating from vertices of degree two and having
83: in their interior only vertices of degree three. Theorem 1.2 is a corollary of Theorem 5.2:
84: using an approach inspired by the discharging method from topological graph theory, we
85: argue that a hypothetical critical graph with girth at least five and average degree less than
86: three always contains a windmill.
87:     For our proof of Theorem 5.2, we define a class of graphs which we call “decent”, which
88: have the property that they cannot be contained in a critical graph. More importantly, we
933: good edge-labelings, or even decent edge-labelings. In this paper, we have used this ap-
934: proach together with a degree-bound. It seems probable that high girth benefits other open
935: problems about good edge-labeling. For example, Araújo et al. [2] conjecture that for every
936: c < 4, the number of (pairwise non-isomorphic) critical graphs with average degree at most
937: c is finite. We propose the following weakening of their conjecture.
938: Conjecture 7.1. For every c < 4, the number of (pairwise non-isomorphic) critical graphs
939: with girth at least five and average degree at most c is finite.
940:    In a follow-up paper, we will use the techniques developed in this paper to prove Con-
941: jecture 7.1 for c = 3.
942:    A more basic question presents itself at this point. As mentioned in the introduction, no
943: examples of bad graphs with girth at least five are known. We pose the following question.
944: Question 7.2. Is there a constant g such that every graph with girth at least g has a good
945: edge-labeling?
946:   We are currently working on constructing bad graphs with girth five. Moreover, we do
947: not believe that g := 6 is sufficient for Question 7.2, and we pose as a third problem to
948: construct bad graphs of girth six.

1174:     A second question is whether one can apply our techniques to get minimal objects. Recall that the
1175: size of the objects we achieve is only minimal up to polynomial factors. For example, one of the main
1176: open problems in design theory is whether there exists a Steiner system (i.e. a t-design with λ = 1) for
1177: any t > 5. Another major open problem of a similar spirit is the existence of Hadamard matrices of all
1178: orders n = 4m, or equivalently, 2-(4m − 1, 2m − 1, m − 1) designs. Empirical estimates for n ≤ 32 suggest
1179: that there are exp(O(n(log n))) Hadamard matrices of order n = 4m. Since are so many of them, and since
1180: the logarithm of their number grows at a regular rate, we suspect that they exist for some purely statistical

1220: ture 7.1. However, it seems to be difficult to prove Conjecture 7.2. In [35], Grone and
1221: Merris only showed that λn−1 (G) ≥ d∗n−1 , in other words, the first and last inequalities
1222: in the majorization inequality hold. In 2002, Duval and Reiner [20] investigated the

1224: plicial complex. They proposed a generalization of Conjecture 7.2 and only proved

1234: Moreover, there are more and more evidence to indicate that Conjecture 7.2 may
1235: hold. For example, Merris in [67] studied the relations between spectra and struc-
1236: ture for a class of graphs which are called degree maximal graphs and found that
1237: (λ1 (G), · · · , λn (G)) = (d∗1 , · · · , d∗n ). In other words, equality in Conjecture 7.2 holds.
1238: Hammer and Klemans in [43] investigated the question of which graphs have integer
1239: spectra and found that the threshold graphs are Laplacian integer. In fact, the degree
1240: maximal graphs are exactly the threshold graphs. It is known that Conjecture 7.2
1241: holds for regular graphs and nearly regular graphs whose vertices have degree either
1242: k or k −1. In 2004, Stephen [86] showed that Conjecture 7.2 holds for trees. However,
1243: up to now, this Conjecture has still not been proved or disproved.

2:                                               We establish a new simple explicit description of combinatorial wall-crossing for the rational
3:                                            Cherednik algebra applied to the trivial representation. In this way we recover a theorem of
4:                                            P. Dimakis and G. Yue in arXiv:1711.05006. We also present two conjectures on combinatorial
5: 
6: 
7: 
8: 
9: arXiv:1809.07206v1 [math.CO] 19 Sep 2018
10:                                            wall-crossing which were found using computer experiments.

15:                                                                                           1
30:     We remark that Loseu proved in [L] that wall-crossing functors are represented by Mullineux
31: transposed on the level of the K-group. Wall-crossing functors were studied in [BL]. The motivation
32: for considering the above permutation comes from Bezrukavnikov’s combinatorial conjecture (see
33: Appendix A in [DY]) which relates wall-crossing functors to monodromy of the quantum connection.
34: More precisely, Bezrukavnikov conjectured that certain invariants (corresponding to dimensions of
35: supports of simple representations of the rational Cherednik algebra) of the composition of the above
36: involutions are equal to similar invariants for a composition of involutions which are much simpler,
37: in particular do not involve the Mullineux involution in their definition.
38:     The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we find a new description of partitions obtained
39: by applying this algorithm to the trivial representation, which were studied in Section 3 of [DY], and
40: using this description we prove a statement equivalent to the Main Theorem of [DY]. In Section 3
41: we conjecture the effect of this algorithm on the sign representation for a prime value of the size of
42: the partition. Finally, in Section 4 we conjecture the inductive structure of this algorithm by means
43: of adding one good box.

47: and Panagiotis Dimakis and Guangyi Yue for many useful discussions and ideas. The author was
48: supported by an NSF Postdoctoral Fellowship.

51: 2    Trivial representation
52: The Farey sequence for n is the sequence of all rational numbers between 0 and 1 which have
53: denominator at most n. We place all Young diagrams in the fourth quadrant (i.e. the quadrant with
116:                                                                                                     for the partition
117: of the integer f1 g1 + · · · + fm gm with gi parts equal to fi .
118: Conjecture 1. There is an increasing sequence of fractions abii in the Farey series such that for
119: ai       ai+1                                               t y
120: bi < r < bi+1 the partition Nr (sgnn ) of n is of the form z x where x, y, z, t satisfy
121:    (a) z + y = bi
122:    (b) y + t + z − x = bi+1
123: Remark 2. Together with the equation xy + zt = n, the above two equations form a system of
124: three equations with four unknowns to be solved over the integers, which is equivalent to one linear
125: Diophantine equation xbi − y(bi+1 − bi ) + bi (bi+1 − bi ) = n with two unknowns.
126: Remark 3. One can begin proving the conjecture by noticing that Mullineux’s original definition
127: of the bi -Mullineux involution uses the notion of bi -rim of a partition. From Conjecture 1 it follows
128: that the bi+1 -rim of the partition Nr (sgnn ) is obtained from the usual rim of Nr (sgnn ) by removing
129: the top row, and the bi -rim of the transposed partition Nr (sgnn )t is obtained from the usual rim
130: of Nr (sgnn )t by removing the top row. Furthermore, after removing the first bi+1 -rim, the Young
131: diagram still has at most two possible lengths of rows.

134: 4     Added good box conjecture
135: The following was checked using a computer program for Young diagrams of size at least 40:

21:                                           We study an algorithm for combinatorial wall-crossing for the rational Cherednik
22:                                                                                   a′           a′
23:                                           algebra. More precisely, let 0 < b′1 < ... < b′k < a/b be all the rational
24:                                                                                    1            k
25:                                           numbers with denominator at most n between 0 and a/b, which we call ”walls,”
26:                                           i.e. the terms of the n-th Farey sequence which are smaller than a/b. Let
27:                                           Md be the generalized Mullineux involution (see Section 2) on the set of d-
40:                                           Negut ([GN]) and Su, Zhao, and Zhong ([SZZ]).
41:                                               The motivation for considering the above permutation M       fa/b comes from
42:                                           Bezrukavnikov’s combinatorial conjecture (see Section 2) which stems from re-
43:                                           lations between wall-crossing functors and monodromy of the quantum connec-
44:                                           tion for the Hilbert scheme of points in the plane (cf. [BO]). More precisely,
45:                                           Bezrukavnikov conjectured that certain invariants (corresponding to dimensions
46:                                           of supports of simple representations of the rational Cherednik algebra) of the

84:     The note is organized as follows. In Section 2 we state the preliminaries,
85: in particular describe Bezrukavnikov’s conjecture. In Section 3 we find a new
86: description of partitions obtained by applying the above algorithm to the trivial
87: representation, and using this description we prove a statement equivalent to
88: the main theorem of [DY]. In Section 4 we conjecture the effect of the algorithm
89: on the sign representation for a prime value of the size of the partition. Finally,
90: in Section 5 we conjecture the inductive structure of this algorithm by means
91: of adding one good box.

128:     To state Bezrukavnikov’s conjecture, we need to define a new operation M  f′
129:                                                                                  a/b
130: on partitions of n for a given term a/b of the n-th Farey sequence. First we define
131: the operation of concatenation ∪ of two partitions as follows: the sequence of
132: rows of the new partition is the multiset union of the two sequences of rows of
133: the old partitions. Next we write our partition as a concatenation µ = ν ∪ dρ
134: where each row of ν is not divisible by d and each row of dρ is divisible by d, and
135: is in fact equal to d times the corresponding row of the partition ρ. Note that
136: there is a unique way to write a partition in this way as a concatenation of two
137: partitions, one of which has all rows not divisible by d and the other one has all
138: rows divisible by d. Finally we define Md′ (µ) = ν∪dρt and M f′ = M ′′ ◦· · ·◦M ′′ ,
139:                                                                a/b     b k       b 1
143: most n between 0 and a/b.
144:    A partition is called d-regular if none of its parts is repeated at least d times.
145: In the statement of Bezrukavnikov’s conjecture the following extension of the
146: Mullineux involution Md (where we also denote the extension by the same letter
147: Md ) from the set of d-regular partitions to the set of all partitions is used. For
148: a partition ρ denote by d ∗ ρ the partition in which each part of ρ is repeated d
149: times. Writing µ = ν ∪ d ∗ ρ with ν regular, we define Md (µ) = Md (ν) ∪ d ∗ ρt .
150: Now we are ready to state (see the definition of M  fa/b in the introduction):
