311: 4     Sign representation
312: Let n be a prime number and let sgnn be the partition of n corresponding
313: to the sign representation of the symmetric group Sn , i.e. its Young diagram
314: is a column of height n. Let r be a term in the n-th Farey sequence. Given
315: positive integers f1 < · · · < fm and g1 , . . . , gm , we will write f1g1 f2g2 . . . fm
316:                                                                                        gm
317:                                                                                           for
318: the partition of the integer f1 g1 + · · · + fm gm with gi parts equal to fi .
319: Conjecture 4.1. There is an increasing sequence of fractions abii in the Farey
320: series such that for ai < r < ai+1 the partition M
321:                         bi          bi+1
322:                                                  fr (sgnn ) of n is of the form
323: z t xy where x, y, z, t satisfy
324:      (a) z + y = bi
325:      (b) y + t + z − x = bi+1
326: Remark 4.2. Together with the equation xy + zt = n, the above two equations
327: form a system of three equations with four unknowns to be solved over the inte-
328: gers, which is equivalent to one linear Diophantine equation xbi − y(bi+1 − bi ) +
329: bi (bi+1 − bi ) = n with two unknowns.
330: Remark 4.3. One can begin proving the conjecture by noticing that Mullineux’s
331: original definition of the bi -Mullineux involution uses the notion of bi -rim of
332: a partition. From Conjecture 4.1 it follows that the bi+1 -rim of the partition
333: fr (sgnn ) is obtained from the usual rim of M
334: M                                             fr (sgnn ) by removing the top row,
335:                                             f
336: and the bi -rim of the transposed partition Mr (sgnn )t is obtained from the usual
337: rim of M fr (sgnn )t by removing the top row. Furthermore, after removing the
338: first bi+1 -rim, the Young diagram still has at most two possible lengths of rows.

177: 
178: Conjecture 2.3. Two consecutive patterns of length k in inversion sequences are strongly Wilf

FILE arxiv.org_pdf_2203.03705v1.txt
17: 
18:                                         1    Introduction
19:                                         In 1989, Babai, Kantor, and Lubotzky made a conjecture that significantly guided research on
20:                                         expander graphs:
21: 
22:                                                                                                    N
23:                                         Conjecture 1.1. ([BKL89].) There are constants k ∈ and λ < 1 such that for every nonabelian
24:                                         finite simple group G, there is a symmetric set S ⊂ G of 2k generators such that the Cayley graph
25:                                         Cay(G, S) is a λ-spectral expander graph.
26: 
27:                                         (Here we say that a graph is a λ-spectral expander if all the eigenvalues of its random walk matrix,
28:                                         excluding the largest, are at most λ.)
29:                                             Notable achievements toward the conjecture include: Kassabov’s proof [Kas07] for the alternat-
30:                                         ing groups; work of Lubotzky and Nikolov [KLN06] proving the conjecture for non-Suzuki groups of
31:                                         Lie type (the Chevalley groups and their twisted versions); and, the Breuillard–Green–Tao [BGT11]
32:                                         proof for the Suzuki groups. In light of the Classification of Finite Simple Groups [Asc04], these
33:                                         completed the proof of Conjecture 1.1. An immediate consequence is that for every nonabelian
34:                                         simple group G, there is a 2k-regular λ-spectral expander K such that G acts transitively on the
35:                                         vertices of K.
36:                                             Having expander graphs with such nontrivial symmetry properties (or even stronger ones) has
37:                                         played an important role in applications to computer science. For example, motivated by the
38:                                         search for locally testable codes (see [KS08]), Kaufman and Wigderson [KW16] made substantial
39:                                         progress on finding so-called “highly symmetric” LDPC codes of constant rate and relative distance
40:                                         (“good”) using expanding Cayley graphs of nonabelian groups; at the same time, they showed that
41:                                         highly symmetric LDPC codes arising from abelian — or even solvable — groups cannot work.
42:                                         Later, notable work of Kaufman and Lubotzky [KL12] (see also [Bec16]) positively resolved the
43:                                         problem, giving explicit, highly symmetric, good LDPC codes; the main tool was the use of explicit
44:                                         edge-transitive (not just vertex-transitive), highly expanding (indeed, Ramanujan) Cayley graphs
62: etry [FGL+ 12], inapproximability [AJT19, DFHT21], list-decoding [AJQ+ 20, DHK+ 21], Markov
63: chain mixing [AL20], property testing [DK17, DD19, KM20, KO20], and quantum codes [EKZ20,
64: KT21]; particularly notable examples including the resolution of the Mihail–Vazirani Conjecture
65: on the bases-exchange walk for matroids [ALOV19] and the construction of locally testable codes
66: of constant rate, distance, and locality [DEL+ 21, PK21].
67: 
68: 1.1    Our goal
69: In this paper, we investigate a problem similar to Conjecture 1.1 for high-dimensional expanders.
70: Namely, for nonabelian finite simple groups G, we seek:
71:    1. bounded-degree λ-spectral HDXs whose top-dimensional faces are acted on transitively by G,
72:    2. with λ arbitrarily close to 0, as opposed to merely bounded away from 1.
73: (Recall that existence of highly symmetric good LDPC codes was resolved by obtaining one-
74: dimensional HDXs — i.e., expander graphs — with both properties.) The aforementioned HDXs
75: built from Bruhat–Tits buildings [Li04, LSV05b, LSV05a, Sar04] have property (2) above, and the
76: work of Kaufman and Lubotzky [KL12] also verified property (1) for G = PSL3 ( ) (for char suf-  F            F
77: ficiently large). Later, Kaufman and Oppenheim [KO18] gave a new (and elementary) construction
78: of HDX families of any dimension d satisfying both (1) and (2) with G = PSLd+1 ( ). These two       F
79: constructions are the only previous examples of bounded-degree λ-spectral HDXs of which we are
80: aware. To quote the final remark from [LSV05b]: “Of course one hopes eventually to define and
317: expansion of vertex links in three different 2-dimensional complexes, two of which are the examples
318: in Section 1.3.
319:     We conclude with further questions. It is interesting to ask if our analogue of Conjecture 1.1
320: has an affirmative answer when G is a more “combinatorial” group; for example, the symmet-
321: ric/alternating group. We also leave open the case of the Chevalley group based on root system G2 ;
322: we conjecture it has the desired expansion properties, and suggest an approach to proving this.
323: 
324:

326: Let   N                 Z                                                           F

328:                                     F                                                F

802:    We have already noted that if p and q are trivially equivalent, then they are strongly Wilf-
803:    equivalent. Computations suggest that these are the only strong Wilf-equivalences.
804:    Conjecture 5.2. Two binary words p and q are strongly Wilf-equivalent if and only if they
805:    are trivially equivalent.
806:      We have verified the above conjecture for binary words of length up to 13 using Sage
807:    [23]. There is a natural composition we can associate to a binary word using its runs.
808:    For example, to 110001, we associate the composition (2, 3, 1). Words being trivially
809:    equivalent is equivalent to the associated compositions being equal or reverses of each
810:    other. From the expressions for Bn,p (k) for k ≤ 4 in Section 3, we see that terms such as
811:    the number of 1s in the composition, number of 2s at the start or end (boundary) of the
812:    composition, etc. play a role. One would hope that the composition can be determined
813:    up to reversal if enough such terms play a role in expressions for higher values of k.

129: which are adjacent to a fixed representative of orbit Oi

131: bigger in the oriented case, because the dimension of the cones OMCUTn and QMETn is twice
132: those of CUTn and METn , and because oriented multi-cuts with q > 2 are not interior points
133: of the cone, denoted by OCUTn , generated by oriented cuts. So-called combinatorial explosion
134: starts from n = 5 (see Table 1) while for corresponding semi-metric cones it starts from n = 7.

105: 1   Introduction
161:                                  cT,U = h PT , PU i                         (2)
162: 
163: This question leads us to conjecture the existence of a tower of algebras over
164: C: A0 ⊂ A1 ⊂ · · · ⊂ An ⊂ · · · with dimC (An ) = n!, whose simple modules
165: ST (and, accordingly, the indecomposable projective modules PT ) would be
166: indexed by the binary trees of size n. To get the complete analogy with the
167: classical case, one should also describe the embeddings Ap ⊗ Aq ֒→ Ap+q and
168: identify PBT and PBT∗ as the direct sums of the Grothendieck groups
169:                           M                           M
170:                      G=         G0 (An ) ,       K=         K0 (An )        (3)
171:                           n≥0                         n≥0
172: 
173: in such a way that the products of PBT and PBT∗ written in the appropriate
174: basis would correspond to the induction process from Ap ⊗ Aq to Ap+q .
176: Up to a simple reodering of the matrix, we propose a good candidate for the
177: matrices of Cartan invariants of a tower of algebras associated with PBT
178: and present a precise conjecture on the induction process, suggested by some
179: new combinatorial properties of binary trees arising in the analysis of these
180: matrices.
181: 
182: This paper is organized as follows: the second Section recalls the basic defini-
183: tions and some classical combinatorial algorithms. In Section 3, we present the
184: algebra of planar binary trees and explain how it leads to the definition of the
185: sylvester monoid. We then present how one can recover the basic properties
186: of PBT in our setting. In Section 4, we build new bases and derive further
187: properties of PBT. Section 5 details the outcome of our representation theo-
188: retical investigation of PBT. Finally, Section 7 gives some transition matrices
189: between different bases of PBT.

13:                                                          We prove that the bases of the matroid Tn,3 characterize the trian-
14:                                                      gles with holes which can be tiled with unit rhombi. More generally,
15:                                                      we provide evidence for a conjectural connection between the matroid
16:                                                      Tn,d , the triangulations of the product of simplices ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 , and
17:                                                      the arrangements of d tropical hyperplanes in tropical (n − 1)-space.
18:                                                          Our work provides a simple and effective criterion to ensure the
19:                                                      vanishing of many Schubert structure constants in the flag manifold,
20:                                                      and a new perspective on Billey and Vakil’s method for computing the
21:                                                      non-vanishing ones.

76: sions. Finally, in Section 8, we prove that each pure mixed subdivision of the
77: Minkowski sum n∆d−1 (or equivalently, each triangulation of the product of
78: simplices ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 ) gives rise to a basis of Tn,d . We conjecture that ev-
79: ery basis of Tn,d arises in this way. In fact, we conjecture that every basis of
80: Tn,d arises from a regular subdivision or, equivalently, from an arrangement
81: of d tropical hyperplanes in tropical (n − 1)-space.

927: Conjecture 7.1. The possible locations of the simplices in a fine mixed
928: subdivision of n∆d−1 are precisely the bases of the matroid Tn,d .
929:      In the remainder of this section, we will give a completely combinatorial
930: description of the fine mixed subdivisions of n∆d−1 . Then, in Section 8,
931: we will use this description to prove Proposition 8.2, which is the forward
932: direction of Conjecture 7.1.
933:      We start by recalling the one-to-one correspondence between the fine
934: mixed subdivisions of n∆d−1 and the triangulations of ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 . This
935: equivalent point of view has the drawback of bringing us to a higher-dimen-
936: sional picture. Its advantage is that it simplifies greatly the combinatorics
937: of the tiles, which are now just simplices.
938:      Let v1 , . . . , vn and w1 , . . . , wd be the vertices of ∆n−1 and ∆d−1 , so that
939: the vertices of ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 are of the form vi × wj . A triangulation T of
940: ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 is given by a collection of simplices. For each simplex t in
941: T , consider the fine mixed cell whose i-th summand is wa wb . . . wc , where
942: a, b, . . . , c are the indexes j such that vi × wj is a vertex of t. These fine
943: mixed cells constitute the fine mixed subdivision of n∆d−1 corresponding to
1070: Having given a combinatorial characterization of the triangulations of the

1221:      For the converse of Conjecture 7.1, we would need to show that every
1222: basis of Tn,d arises from a fine mixed subdivision of n∆d−1 . In fact, we
1223: conjecture a stronger result, which we state after introducing the necessary
1224: definitions.
1225:      Recall the definition of a regular subdivision of a polytope given in Sec-
1226: tion 7. Similarly, a regular mixed subdivision of a Minkowski sum P1 +· · ·+Pn
1227: in Rd is obtained by assigning a height hi (v) to each vertex v of Pi , and pro-
1228: jecting the lower facets of the convex hull of the points in Rd+1 of the form
1229: (v1 , h1 (v1 )) + · · · + (vn , hn (vn )), where vi is a vertex of Pi .

1231: Conjecture 8.3. For any basis B of Tn,d , there is a regular fine mixed
1232: subdivision of n∆d−1 whose n simplices are located at B.

1234:     The Cayley trick provides us with a bijection between the triangulations
1235: of ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 and the fine mixed subdivisions of n∆d−1 . This correspon-
1236: dence also gives a bijection between regular triangulations of ∆n−1 × ∆d−1
1237: and regular fine mixed subdivisions of n∆d−1 .[15, Theorem 3.1] There is also
1238: a correspondence between the regular triangulations of ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 and
1239: the combinatorial types of arrangements of d generic tropical hyperplanes
1240: in tropical (n − 1)-space [8, 31].
1241:     Just as the combinatorial properties of real hyperplane arrangements are
1242: captured in the theory of oriented matroids, tropical hyperplane arrange-
1247: multiple appearances of these subdivisions in the literature are presumably
1248: a good indication of the applicability of tropical oriented matroid theory.
1249: Our ability to attack Conjectures 7.1 and 8.3 is one way to measure our
1250: progress on this theory.

1255: 9       Applications to Schubert calculus.
1256: In this section, we show some of the implications of our work in the Schubert
1257: calculus of the flag manifold. Throughout this section, we will assume some
1258: familiarity with the Schubert calculus, though we will recall some of the
1259: definitions and conventions that we will use; for more information, see for
1260: example [11, 22]. We will also need some of the results of Eriksson and Li-
1581:   troids, appear in many different contexts. A detailed investigation of
1582:   these objects promises to become a useful tool. Aside from their in-
1583:   trinsic interest, Conjectures 7.1 and 8.3 should help us develop this
1584:   tropical oriented matroid theory.
1585: 
1586: • We still do not have a solid understanding of the relationship between
1587:   two of the main subjects of our paper: the geometry of d flags in Cn
1588:   and the triangulations of ∆n−1 × ∆d−1 . We have shown that some
1589:   aspects of the geometric information of the flags (the combinatorics
1590:   of the line arrangement they determine, and the vanishing of many
1591:   Schubert structure constants) are described in a small set of tiles of the
1592:   triangulations (the n “pure” tiles). Can we use the complete triangula-
1593:   tions and fine mixed subdivisions to understand more subtle geometric
1594:   questions about flags? Does the geometry of flags tell us something

428: 
429: Extremal Partition Theory
430: 
431: Since each row of a binary covering array corresponds to a set, two results from
432: extremal set theory, Sperner’s Theorem and the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem, can be
433: used to find the exact size of the smallest binary covering array, CA(n, r, 2) for all
434: values of r. The famous Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem [24] is concerned with the maximal
435: cardinality of intersecting set systems as well as with the structure of such maximal
436: 
437: CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                 5
459: intersecting partition system is a trivially intersecting system. Another notion of in-
460: tersecting partitions is partial intersection. Two partitions are partially t-intersecting
461: if they have classes with intersection of cardinality at least t. We conjecture a version
462: of the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem for partially intersecting partitions, that is, a trivially
463: partially intersecting partition system is the largest partially intersecting partition
464: system. This conjecture is proven in certain restricted cases in Section 7.5. Different
465: extensions of the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem to systems of intersecting partitions have
466: also been considered by P. L. Erdős, Székely and László [25].
467: 
468: General extremal partition systems
469: 
470: Higher order extremal problems are extremal problems in which the elements in the
471: system are disjoint families of subsets of a finite set called clouds, rather than sets.
472: Ahlswede, Cai and Zhang [3] present a framework for several different types of higher
473:

508: for extremal partition systems is described. In Chapter 9, we list open questions and
509: conjectures that arise from this study. Finally, in Appendix A we present a table of

667: N(n) ≥ 1 for all n, this is not a useful result. It is non-trivial to conclude that it is
668: impossible to build a pair of MOLS of order 6 (indeed, N(6) = 1, see Section 6.4 [73]).
669: Euler conjectured that N(n) = 1 for all n ≡ 2 (mod 4). This conjecture was proven

673: N(3) = 2, N(6) = 1 and N(10) ≥ 2, and for all other values of n, N(n) ≥ 3 [73].

3019: 5.2.3    Homomorphisms and Qualitative Independence Graphs
3020: Recall from Section 4.1.2, that a proper colouring with n colours of a graph G is
3021: equivalent to a homomorphism from G to the complete graph Kn . We give a similar
3022: characterization for covering arrays on graphs.
3079:                     χ(G) ≤ χ(QI(n, k)) and ω(G) ≤ ω(QI(n, k)).
3080: 
3081:    With Theorem 5.2.7, Conjecture 5.2.6 can be rephrased in terms of homomor-
3082: phisms.
3083: 
3084: Conjecture 5.2.11.     For all integers n, k, there is no homomorphism QI(n, k) →
3085: QI(n − 1, k).
3086: 
3087:    It is in general difficult to prove that no homomorphism exists between two graphs.
3088: One method to prove this would be to show either χ(QI(n, k)) > χ(QI(n − 1, k)) or
3089: ω(QI(n, k)) > ω(QI(n − 1, k)).
3090:    With this motivation, we try to find bounds and exact values for χ(QI(n, k)) and
3091: ω(QI(n, k)).

3672: 6.1       Uniform Qualitative Independence Graphs
3673: Recall from Section 3.1.2 that, for positive integers n, k, c with n = ck, a uniform
3674: k-partition of an n-set is a partition of an n-set into k classes each of size c.
3675: Definition 6.1.1 (Uniform Qualitative Independence Graph). For positive
3676: integers n, k, with the property that k divides n and n ≥ k 2 , the uniform qualitative
3677: independence graph UQI(n, k) is the graph whose vertex set is the set of all uniform k-
3678: partitions of an n-set. Vertices are adjacent if and only if the corresponding partitions
3679: are qualitatively independent.

4843: higher order extremal problems. Ahlswede, Cai and Zhang [3] give a good overview of

5990: Proof. Assume P is a partially t-intersecting c-uniform k-partition system in Pkck with
5991: t ≥ c − k + 2. Note that P ⊆ V (UQI(ck, k)).
5992:     To show P is an independent set in UQI(ck, k) we will show that for any P, Q ∈
5993: V (UQI(ck, k)), P and Q are not qualitatively independent. Since P and Q are
5994: partially t-intersecting there are classes P1 ∈ P and Q1 ∈ Q such that |P1 ∩ Q1 | ≥ t.
6013: ✰
6014: 
6015:     We can prove the bound in Conjecture 7.5.2 for specific partition systems; to do
6016: this we need the following theorem.
6017: 
6018: CHAPTER 7. PARTITION SYSTEMS                                                             131

6022: Theorem 7.5.5. For positive integers n, k, c and t with n = ck and t ≤ c, if there
6023: exists a resolvable t-(n, c, 1) design, then the size of the largest partially t-intersecting
6024: uniform k-partition system from Ukn is bounded above by
6025:                                                
6026:                1      n−t n−c             c      n−t
6128: This bound is attained by a trivially partially 2-intersecting k-partition system.
6129:    It is interesting to note that Lemma 6.3.3 was proven with the ratio bound and
6130: the eigenvalues of QI(k 2 , k). It may be possible to prove Conjecture 7.5.2 using the
6131: eigenvalues of the graph P IP (n, k) and the ratio bound.
6132: Question 7.5.10.       Is it possible to find the eigenvalues of P IP (n, k)? Does the
6133: ratio bound (Theorem 4.2.12) give a good bound on α(P IP (n, k))?

6136: 2324, 2380, 2540, 2740, 2812, 3620, 3820, 6356}, the largest partially 3-intersecting uni-

6517: CHAPTER 8. HIGHER ORDER PROBLEMS                                                               143
6518: 
6519: 
6520: 
6521:    Ahlswede, Cai and Zhang [3] conjecture that the largest system of c-uniform clouds
6522:                                                            
6523: which is intersecting with type (∃, ∀) has cardinality n−1
6524:                                                        c−1
6525:                                                             . In [1] this conjecture is
6526: shown to be false; they give a system of clouds that is larger than this bound for
6527: all c ≥ 8. The counter example given in [1] requires that each cloud in the system
6528: contain intersecting sets.
6529: 
6530: Bounds for Dn∗ (∃, ∃, k) and In∗ (∀, ∀, k) where k ≤ c
6531: 
6532: The next pair to consider is In∗ (∀, ∀, k) and Dn∗ (∃, ∃, k). Two partitions are intersecting
6533: of type (∀, ∀) if and only if they are qualitatively independent. Thus, the graph
6534: GIn∗ (∀,∀,k) is exactly the graph UQI(n, k). The value of In∗ (∀, ∀, k) is the size of the
6535: maximum clique in the uniform qualitative independence graph. This is not an easy
6536: question; perhaps, trying to find the value of Dn∗ (∃, ∃, k) might be easier or at least
6753: an extension of Sperner’s Theorem to partition systems. We prove that, for integers
6754: n and k where k divides n, the largest Sperner partition system in Pkn is a uniform
6755: partition system. We also conjecture, for all values of n and k, that the largest
6756: Sperner partition system in Pkn is an almost-uniform partition system. Unlike the
6757: case for k = 2, this extension of Sperner’s Theorem to partition systems is not
6758: enough to prove the existence of a homomorphism from QI(n, k) to UQI(n, k). It
6759: would be interesting to know if the largest qualitatively independent partition system
6760: is a uniform (or an almost-uniform) partition system. This would be true if there is
6761: a homomorphism from QI(n, k) to UQI(n, k) (or to AUQI(n, k)).
6762:    There are two ways to extend the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem to partition systems.
6763: For the first extension, we define two partitions to be intersecting if they have a class
6764: in common. We prove that the largest uniform partition system with this type of
6765: intersection is a trivially intersecting system. For the second extension, we consider
6766: a different type of intersection called partial intersection. This type of intersection is
6770: 
6771: 
6772: related to independent sets in qualitative independence graphs. We conjecture that
6773: the largest partially t-intersecting partition system is a trivially partially t-intersecting
6774: partition system. We can prove this conjecture for partially t-intersecting partition
6775: systems from Ukn for many specific values of t, n and k. For example, a partially 2-
6776:                                       2
6777: intersecting partition system in Ukk is equivalent to an independent set in the graph
6778: QI(k 2 , k). Using the eigenvalues of QI(k 2 , k), we prove that a trivially partially 2-
6779: intersecting partition system is the largest partially 2-intersecting partition system in
6780:    2
6781: Ukk .
6782:        There are many open problems related to the extension of Sperner’s Theorem and
6783: the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem to partition systems. First, it would be nice to find
6784: a tighter connection between Sperner’s Theorem for partition systems, the Erdős-
6785: Ko-Rado Theorem for partition systems and covering arrays (see Question 9.1.14).
6786: Second, it would be interesting to know the exact bound for n in the Erdős-Ko-Rado
6787: Theorem for partition systems (see Theorem 7.2.4) and to find an extension of the
6788: complete Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem for partition systems (see Conjecture 7.4.2). Wil-
6789: son’s proof [76] of the exact bound for n in the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem used the ratio
6790: bound, Lemma 4.2.10, with the eigenvalues of the Johnson scheme (Example 4.2.9,
6791: Section 4.2.3). Is it possible to prove a complete Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem for inter-
6792: secting partition systems (or partially intersecting partitions) in a similar manner?
6793: Finally, it would be better to have a version of the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem for all
6794: partially intersecting partition systems, rather than the collection of specific cases
6795: (see Corollaries 7.5.6, 7.5.7, and 7.5.8).
6796:        These extensions of Sperner’s Theorem and the Erdős-Ko-Rado Theorem to parti-
6797: tion systems are a starting point for the study of extremal partition theory. Through-
6798: out the thesis, we give motivation for why these problems are interesting and in Chap-
6799: ter 8, we present a framework in which to consider a variety of extremal problems for
6801: we give a bound on the cardinality of the maximum system. There are still many
6802: problems to solve and other possible extensions to be considered. We conclude with
6803: a list of questions followed by several conjectures.
6804:

6813: CAN(r, k). Can this construction be extended or generalized to give a good upper
6814: bound for CAN(r, k) for r ≥ 2k? This construction uses a “starter vector” which is

89:                       2. Continuity of tM (x), x ≥ 0
90:   In [4], the authors consider intervals of the form I = [0, b], where, for

100:    To study the behaviour of tM (x), it is useful to look back at the classical
101: paper [2] of Borwein and Erdélyi in the theory of the (non-monic) transfinite
102: diameter. In this paper, the authors define the function tZ (x) in the equivalent

221:   The continuity of tM (x) sheds some light on conjectures made by Hare and
222: Smyth in [4]:
223: Conjecture 1 (Zero-endpoint interval conjecture).
224:                                                  If I = [0, b] with b ≤ 1,
225:               1                      1
226: then tM (I) = n , where n = max 2, ⌈ b ⌉ is the smallest integer n ≥ 2 for
227: which n1 ≤ b.
228: Conjecture 2 (Maximal obstruction implies tM (I) conjecture). If an interval
229: I of length less than 4 has a maximal obstruction m(I), then tM (I) = m(I).
230:    Theorem 1 clearly shows Conjecture 1 to be false: since tM (x) is continuous
231: to the left of n1 , n ∈ N and tM ([0, n1 ]) = n1 , we cannot have tM ([0, b]) = n+1
232:                                                                                  1
233: 
234: for all b < n1 , as claimed in the conjecture. A further implication is that for
235: these intervals, tM (I) 6= m(I), contrary to Conjecture 2.

369:                   4. The Farey interval conjecture
370:   Another open conjecture, this one taken from [3], is the following:
371: Conjecture 3 (Farey Interval Conjecture). Let pq , rs ∈ Q with q, s > 0 be
372: such that rq − ps = 1. Then
373:                                             
374:                             p r             1 1
375:                       tM     ,     = max     ,       .
376:                             q s             q s
377:    Computationally, the authors verified the conjecture for denominators up
378: to 21 and it was proved for an infinite family of such intervals in [4]. It
379: is perhaps worth noting that continuity of tM (x) cannot be used to find a
380: counterexample to this conjecture, as the following argument shows.
381:    Let n > 1 ∈ N. We will show that we cannot find a Farey Interval of the
382: form [ nk , pq ] with 1 ≤ k < n, n = min{q, n} and pq > b∗max (n), where
383:                                                 
